If the 2005 ontology is granted, strong emergence satisfies a limited property-level reading of conscious fundamentality, even though every instance is causally dependent. But the maintenance condition requires supporting organization. Causal mental influence therefore does nothing to establish brain independence, and the model must reproduce the neural-dependence patterns already in the record before any empirical comparison favors it. A fair cost inventory counts, on each side, basic experiential properties, latent powers and laws, generation and maintenance conditions, and the assignment of qualities and subjects. No inspected original derives a uniquely confirmed theory from those costs.

Panpsychism and experiential constitution

The operational panpsychist comparator bets that experiential constituents can support an account of complex subjects. Its bridge is an explicit constitutive relation that grounds unified subjecthood. Its stipulated objection is that many subjects do not evidently constitute one subject. The developed position in the record is constitutive Russellian panpsychism, as presented in the inspected Chalmers manuscript. A version note is needed at once: that manuscript is an undated author text labeled forthcoming, and the record does not fix its exact published version. On this view experience realizes the intrinsic natures of physical dispositions. Microexperience therefore has causal roles through physics rather than intervening from outside, and macroexperience is grounded in microexperience plus whatever further relations the base provides [S-T12-001]. The attraction is integration. Phenomenality and causal relevance come in one package, without new macro-level laws.

The same inspected text is the source of the family's canonical difficulty. Chalmers separates the combination problem into three components: subject, quality and structure. How do microsubjects make a macrosubject? How do microqualities make macroqualities? How does microstructure make the smooth structure of experience? He argues that solving one of these leaves the others standing. His anti-combination arguments rely on modal premises that he himself marks as disputable, and he treats phenomenal bonding as promising while questioning its nature and boundaries. The project analysis draws the family's outer boundary. An experiential intrinsic-nature model entails neither a subject for every aggregate nor one universal subject.

The record's developed positive argument is Strawson's. On the inspected authorial version, realistic monism runs as a conditional argument. Experience is concrete reality. Assume that concrete reality is physical and constituted by ultimates. No experiential reality can emerge from wholly nonexperiential reality. Therefore some ultimates involve experience; that is micropsychism. Panpsychism proper follows only with an added homogeneity premise [S-P2T03-004]. The premise that does the work, no radical emergence, is an intuition the text defends but cannot demonstrate. There is also a version boundary to respect. The selected 2006 journal text was never inspected. The inspected version is the later authorial chapter, and the two are one work-version family, not independent corroboration.

Goff's inspected objection turns Strawson's own weapon against him. Microexperiences do not transparently supply a further subject. So the brute emergence that Strawson forbids between the nonexperiential and the experiential seems to reappear between micro and macro. Furthermore, Strawson uses transparent experiential knowledge against a posteriori physicalist identification, and that threatens to prove too much, since experiential constitution is no more transparent [S-P2T03-002]. The phenomenal-parthood argument sharpens the point. If experiential being is exhaustively how it feels, then a constituent's character must figure in the whole's character, and slight pains do not sum to a severe pain of a new quality. On the stronger transparency and parthood premises, novel experiential combination is not merely unexplained. It is incoherent.

Strawson's inspected reply concedes remarkably much while holding the line. He accepts that Goff's argument refutes one natural full-revelation reading of his position. His answer is a distinction between first-order acquaintance and exhaustive disclosure. Having an experience does not reveal every constitutive fact about it, so the absence of microsubjects from the macrosubject's acquaintance does not establish that they are absent [S-P2T03-003]. Constituting microsubjects are numerically distinct, and they need not share their perspectives with the subject they constitute. Experiences are active realities whose causal powers are internal to their nature, and he explicitly acknowledges this as a revision of his earlier view. The concessions go further. He acknowledges a defective argument in his own text at the recorded locus. He concedes that he has no account of how microexperience constitutes macroexperience. He rests the claim that it does on a transcendental argument plus smallism, and he acknowledges that appeals to prior commitment stand in formal parity on both sides.

Where does this leave us? With restraint on both sides. The exchange does not establish subject combination. Nor does it demonstrate that combination is impossible, except by relying on the disputed revelation and parthood bridges. An epistemic defense does not discharge the positive burden of showing how constitution works. And the family's reach is bounded on its own terms. Micropsychism and experiential constitution entail no cosmic subject, no surviving person and no brain-independent human consciousness. Strawson himself accepts the relevant experiential states as brain states within his account, and he treats experience as requiring time.

Substance dualism

The operational dualist comparator posits distinct, irreducible bearers of subjectivity that stand in coherent and constrained relations to bodies. Its stipulated objection is conflict with a complete physical causal account. The developed position is Lowe's non-Cartesian substance dualism, on the inspected article. The self is a persisting bearer of properties, distinct from its body and its parts. Lowe motivates that distinctness by considerations about replacement and about what bears thoughts, and he makes no commitment to the possibility of disembodiment [S-P2T02-001]. His causal proposal turns on a distinction between intentional fact causation and neural event causation. The self causes the fact that some appropriate neural events occur, while the physical event chain remains complete. Closure is conceded conditionally, for the argument's sake.

The recorded challenges are two. Engelhardt argues that the sufficiency of physical events supports the sufficiency of occurrence facts, which reinstates exclusion against Lowe's fact-causation distinction [S-P2T02-002]. A version note applies. The Engelhardt text was inspected through an author-linked transcription carrying the printed page markers; the exact publisher text is unverified, and the author's page generally labels its linked works penultimate drafts. Bennett's inspected chapter applies her counterfactual overdetermination test to dualism, conditional on physical completeness. Genuine independent sufficient causes must pass counterfactual independence checks that dependent causal descriptions fail. A dualist with merely contingent psychophysical links therefore needs a response that the physicalist's dependence strategy cannot supply. The exception would be a coherent, motivated necessitating relation between the distinct substances, an option she doubts but does not close [S-P2T02-003].

Engelhardt's own inclusive variant answers by revising the position. Let the distinct mind and body share one causally relevant physical property instance, so that two bearers do not supply two independent physical pushes. And require the counterfactual comparisons to hold a defensible embodiment relation fixed, rather than severing that relation and testing a different situation [S-P2T02-002]. The inspected text is careful about what it claims. It disclaims meeting the independent-powers ambition that it attributes to Lowe's fuller view. Colocation, token property sharing and the embodiment relation stand as substantive burdens. A correlation that is merely named will not carry them.

What has the exchange achieved? It improves the conditional comparison of bearer models and causal models. It does not establish that a distinct mental substance exists. Two boundary inferences matter for later chapters. First, a primitive continuing subject supplies no observed postmortem identity test, so the exchange establishes nothing about survival or timelessness. Second, the exclusion burden concerns sufficient causes, dependence and event individuation. Energy conservation alone does not settle this dispute either way.

Idealism: universal and plural grounds

The operational idealist comparator bets that a conscious basis could ground both experience and a public, ordered world, and that it would earn its support through overall explanatory success. Its stipulated objection is that experiential knowledge does not prove a mental ontology. The record's developed universal version is Kastrup's dissociation-based cosmic idealism. It was inspected as an identified journal-layout version, accessed through a transcription with printed page markers. The model has three parts. There is one cosmic conscious primitive, whose patterns of self-excitation are experiences. Finite private perspectives arise by dissociation, located at the boundaries of living organisms. And bodies and brains are the extrinsic appearances of dissociated experiential processes, with cross-boundary impingement and coded perception explaining the perceived world [S-T12-002]. The selling point is economy: one ontological kind. The inspected text conditions that economy explicitly on explanatory adequacy.

The record is strict about what supports this model. Kastrup's clinical dissociation findings, his no-report paradigms, his quantum-measurement claims and his biological illustrations all enter the record as attributions; their original studies were not inspected. No clinical or physical fact is accepted from the philosophical paper. The project analyses press two structural points. Dissociation in embodied patients does not by itself establish that a cosmic subject divides. The analogy must ground finite phenomenality, not merely block communication. And to call neural activity an appearance, one must recover exact neural interventions and public regularities; otherwise the data are redescribed rather than explained. A lawful shared mental world is a coherent target for comparison. But specifying a shared substrate does not derive its particular laws.

The recorded serious challenge is Chalmers's conditional subject-constitution objection. It was inspected in draft and final-layout versions, and the record leaves their exact publication history unresolved. The objection runs as follows. A cosmic subject with all its cosmic mental states can seemingly be conceived without any further distinct macrosubjects. If grounding requires necessitation, and if the relevant conceivability entails possibility, then pure cosmic grounding of distinct subjects fails [S-T12-003]. Chalmers also considers the identity reading, on which we are the cosmic subject under restricted modes. He treats this mode-relative cognitive fragmentation as coherent but expensive, in introspective and interpersonal costs. The final layout adds a no-highest-level countercase against cosmic holism. One asymmetry must be noted. The inspected Chalmers text engages Kastrup's earlier paper, not the inspected 2018 article. The confrontation is therefore an argumentative reconstruction, not a verified direct reply chain.

The same material generalizes into the record's comparison of cosmic and plural idealism. Whole-first models relocate the constitution problem rather than automatically solving it. Physical holism alone does not select an experiential ground. Identity versions revise ordinary individuality, and nonidentity versions owe a sufficient grounding principle. Plural idealism, with multiple basic subjects lawfully coordinated, is a recorded alternative, and it permits a mental ontology without one universal subject. A shared bearer, a shared kind of reality and numerical identity are three different claims. Motifs of unity establish none of them.

The record's developed plural comparator is historical, and it was inspected: Leibniz. The Monadology argues from composites to genuine simples. It rejects any mechanical explanation of perception by the mill argument. It equips each monad with an intrinsic representational nature and a qualitative individuality, and it distinguishes bare perception from apperception, so that not every monad is a conscious person [S-T10-002]. One disclosure belongs here. Those Monadology claims were inspected in the 1998 Francks and Woolhouse translation, at printed pages 268 to 270, 274 to 277 and 279. The record carries them under the same source identity as the Bennett modernization at the cited address, whose own inspected sections concern sufficient reason, God and the best world. The Bennett text was not reinspected for the mill argument. Created monads remain plural while depending on God and representing one universe. Ideal coordination and distinct laws for soul and body do not identify created subjects with one mind. The New System supplies the 1695 published statement of substantial unities and divine initial coordination. It was inspected in an identified translation with its later amendments marked [S-P2T04-002].

The inspected challenge is Foucher's 1695 reply, and it presses three points. Coordinated sequences are possible, but why have bodies at all, if appearances are internally sufficient and bodies are unnecessary? A theory built to preserve prior separations may lack independent justification. And interaction by effort and resistance is excluded only by premises a critic need not grant [S-P2T04-003]. Leibniz's actual 1696 published explanation answers on the record. Harmony is grounded in law rather than in perpetual intervention. The foundations of mass differ from abstract extension, and genuine animal unity differs from clockwork. God prefers a plurality of substances with external correspondence. Force is intrinsic, and conservation is invoked against interactionism [S-P2T04-004]. The limits are recorded too: abbreviated translations, an omitted chain of rejoinders, and theological premises of divine actuality, choice and coordination doing essential work.

What do these inspected versions give the family? A developed and bounded comparison: universal and plural mental ontologies, each with explicit mechanisms and explicit costs. They establish none of the following: fundamental or universal consciousness, brain independence, survival, timelessness, preservation, intention, goodness or authority. Leibniz's conclusions about preservation and moral order rest on additional theological premises, and the comparison does not adjudicate them.

Theistic accounts

The operational theistic comparator is built from two bets. Contingent reality may require explanation, and intentional agency may provide it. Its stipulated bridge is unusually demanding, and the project imposed that demand deliberately. Independent arguments are required for agency, for good ends and for moral authority, because explanation alone does not identify mind, goodness or accountability. The stipulated strongest objection is that very gap. The stipulated reply is to supply the distinct arguments, and to test the specified ends against suffering and hiddenness rather than asserting them.

Within this chapter's inputs, the theistic family's developed content is thinner than that of its rivals. This must be said plainly. The family appears here as the project-stipulated comparator, plus the inspected theological commitments inside the Leibniz texts: divine creation, coordination and a preference for plurality. Those commitments enter as attributions within an idealist comparison, not as a defended contemporary theism. The developed positive material bearing on this family, the necessary-foundation arguments, fine-tuning and design, is assigned under the Phase 3 plan to the existence chapter. The moral-grounding material is assigned to the morality and goodness chapters. Those assessments are made in chapters 5, 9 and 10, and none of them is imported into this chapter's comparison. For the comparison in this chapter the consequence is a coverage asymmetry, and it has teeth. There is no primary contemporary theist defender among this set's inspected originals. The recorded constraints therefore bar broad elimination claims against the family, and they equally bar any credit to it beyond its stipulated shape.

Common evaluation criteria and overlap

All six families are assessed under the same recorded conventions. Each declared model carries required, excluded and open commitments. These are classifications of content, not of confidence. Moral and identity positions are never inferred from family membership. The comparison criteria are those of the methodology chapter, operationalized in the project's comparison annex as six checks. The last check is remaining problems: what a position still cannot explain after its best defense. The duty to take the strongest formulation runs symmetrically. The record's illusionism is Frankish with his concessions intact, and the record's dualism is Lowe with the exclusion challenge at full strength.

How do the coverage marks fall across this chapter's inputs? What follows is this chapter's own application of the recorded schema to the versions its records name; it is not the result of a dedicated coverage audit. The inspected original chains of defender, objection and reply are these: the illusionism exchange, the causal-emergence exchange, the panpsychist combination exchange, the substance-dualist exclusion exchange including Engelhardt's interactive inclusive variant, and the Leibniz-Foucher exchange. The inspected originals without full reply chains are Papineau's identity defense; Chalmers's combination analysis, inspected as an undated forthcoming manuscript; Chalmers's idealism analysis, inspected in both draft and final layout with its exact publication history unresolved; and Kastrup's 2018 article. The six operational comparators are project reconstructions. The secondary attributions are Kastrup's clinical and physical premises, the commentator positions seen only through Frankish's replies, and Goff's cited antecedents. The uninspected variants stand just as the records leave them: the exact 2006 Strawson journal text, the 2021 reprint of the Chalmers idealism chapter, and the later rounds of the Shoemaker exchange. Verdicts in later chapters are restricted by these marks.

Dependence crosses family lines, and the record tracks it. Consider the three Chalmers papers, which the record treats as one dependent conceptual lineage. One author's framework supplies the foil for the identity dialectic, the taxonomy of the combination problem and the machinery of the idealism objection. Material used across three of this chapter's sections is therefore partly one thinker's premises, and any cumulative weighing counts it once. Each developed exchange is likewise one dependent unit, however many claims it yields. The illusionism records share a single-exchange dependency group, and so do the emergence, combination, exclusion and Leibniz materials. Shared premises that carry weight are tracked the same way. No radical emergence, the bridges from conceivability to possibility, and grounding as necessitation each enter the record once, as a labeled premise, and never as an added observation. That is the same treatment shared samples receive in the empirical chapters.

One constraint binds every family alike. Reviewed neural dependence constrains each model's mapping to embodied experience, whether the model works by identity, psychophysical law, constitution, distinct substance or appearance-grounding. By itself, however, it decides nothing among them. And one prohibition binds every family's ambitions. None of these family-level arguments automatically supplies personal survival, timelessness, preservation, intentional creation, goodness or moral authority. Those components need their own bridges, argued in their own chapters. Where a component has not been assessed, that absence is never converted into equal support.

What this chapter has not shown

Let me be clear about what this chapter has not shown. This survey establishes a comparison space, not a result. It cannot rank the six families, and it does not. The coverage asymmetries just recorded are the very conditions under which the recorded constraints withhold family rankings. There is an uninspected exact first version at the heart of the panpsychism exchange. There is an undated forthcoming manuscript in the combination material, and an idealism analysis inspected in draft and final layout but with its publication history unresolved. The Kastrup and Engelhardt texts were inspected through transcriptions. The theistic family is present mainly by stipulation. And every one of the twelve withheld stronger-conclusion gates remains withheld at the end of this chapter. None is opened here. Two standing limits frame the whole space. The frozen protocol's own header records that the six families were not source-verified as representative of the literature when they were fixed. And the register's original-defender coverage gap remains open. The five inspected exchanges are selected chains, and the register is explicit that completing later packets does not by itself close gaps in representative coverage. Alternatives that are unassessed or thinly covered are not thereby refuted. Nor are they thereby supported.

What the chapter does deliver, and what later chapters consume, is a disciplined inventory. For each family it gives a strongest recorded formulation with named versions, a strongest recorded challenge met by an actual reply where one exists, explicit bridge premises, explicit costs, and a coverage mark on every text that bears weight. Each of the six families remains a live comparator, and each carries specific unresolved burdens. The physicalist must show that the target is preserved. The psychophysical-law theorist has a brute mapping, and on the family's strong-emergence wing the generation laws are unconfirmed. The panpsychist is silent on constitution. The dualist owes an answer to exclusion. The idealist owes accounts of individuation and lawfulness. The theist has not discharged the agency and goodness bridges. But liveness is not support. A position present mainly by stipulation stands in the comparison without acquiring the standing of the inspected ones, and the absence of assessment is never converted into equal support. No metaphysical conclusion follows from any of this, and none is drawn. All twelve withheld stronger-conclusion gates remain withheld at the close of this chapter.

4What Experiential Primitiveness Explains

The previous chapter set out the positions. This chapter asks what moves a theorist to take experience as basic, and what that move costs. The question is not which family of views is true. That question is gated, and it stays gated. The question is what a theory buys when it counts experience among its primitives, and what debts it takes on in the same transaction. Four exchanges from the record carry the analysis: the explanatory-gap debate between Chalmers and Papineau, the illusionism debate between Frankish and Nida-Rümelin, the causal-emergence debate among O'Connor and Wong, Kim and Shoemaker, and the constitution debate between Strawson and Goff. The chapter closes with the burdens that every conscious-ground proposal takes over, because those burdens shape everything the later, empirical chapters can and cannot test.

One caution governs every sentence that follows. Nothing in this chapter's inputs is an empirical observation. They are inspected philosophical texts, together with reviewed project analyses of those texts. So every "argues," "proposes" and "concedes" below is a documented attribution to an identified version of a text, and an attribution never establishes the truth of what is attributed. Each exchange is also internally dependent. The gap debate and its phenomenal-concepts sequel share authors and a running dispute, and each debate forms a single dependency group (a set of records that counts as one source of support, not several). However many claims this chapter yields, none of them multiplies into independent lines of support.

The demand that survives functional explanation

The demand comes from Chalmers, and the record carries it as a distinction rather than a doctrine. Explaining discrimination, access or behavioral control is one kind of task. Explaining why those processes have a subjective character is another [S-T03-001]. The inspected text is an author-hosted version of the 1995 article, examined at its relevant sections, and it does not claim that functional explanation has achieved nothing. Its claim is conditional and structural: finishing the functional program would not obviously finish the different task of accounting for experience. That conditional is what survives ordinary explanatory success. It is also what every position in this chapter is answering, deflating or refusing.

A theory that includes experience among its primitives meets the demand in one definite sense. It no longer has to make the experiential arise from a vocabulary that was stipulated to contain nothing experiential. That is genuine relief, but it is relief by definition. To decline to derive experience is not yet to explain anything about it: which experiences occur, in which systems, and with what structure. The article frames the matter in a way this thesis keeps. The advantage is real only if the demand has been correctly identified, and that identification is the very thing the physicalist side contests.

The developed antiphysicalist argument in the record runs through knowledge and conceivability. On the author text, inspected at its relevant sections, Chalmers argues against the phenomenal-concept strategy and for a conditional conclusion. If the physical facts can be conceived without the experiential facts, and if the relevant conceivability entails the relevant possibility, then physicalism is false [S-T05-003]. Three bridge principles are recorded with that argument. The modal bridge takes ideal conceivability to relevant metaphysical possibility. The knowledge bridge takes new epistemic access to an ontologically new fact, and the record treats it as an additional requirement of the knowledge argument rather than as part of the conceivability conditional itself. The explanatory bridge is a standard: a physical explanation must make the high-level truth transparently derivable, and one standard must be applied consistently across physical and experiential cases. The record marks all three as contested, and the conclusion as conditional on them. The text itself is an author PDF identified by its exact URL, and its publication year appears as both 2006 and 2007 in the discovery metadata.

The physicalist defense in the record has two prongs. The first is the phenomenal-concept strategy itself. Papineau defends the view that phenomenal and physical concepts are two distinct epistemic routes to one and the same property, so that an epistemic difference, a difference in the way we know, need not refute identity [S-T05-004]. The inspected text is an author-hosted typeset chapter, and the record leaves its exact publication year unresolved. Chalmers answers with a dilemma, in the same dependency group. Roughly, the conceptual features that are supposed to do the explaining either resist physical explanation themselves, or they fail to explain the epistemic situation they were invoked to explain, including its truth and justification. The record leaves that dilemma, like everything else here, as an attributed argument with contested premises.

The second prong attacks the standard of explanation the demand assumes. On the inspected published article, an author-hosted text of the 2011 Philosophia paper, Papineau challenges what an identity owes us. Identities, once warranted, need no further explanation. So the leftover question, "why is this process experienced this way," may be a demand for something no true identity could supply [S-T05-005]. The felt gap is moved out of ontology and into psychology: it becomes a resistance to identification whose causes are open to empirical study. Notice what this deflation does. It does not close the gap; it reclassifies it.

The lesson for method, stated in the article this chapter expands, is that the burden runs both ways. The antireductionist must defend the step from a gap in explanation to a gap in the world. That explanatory bridge is a premise, not a datum. The reductionist must show that the proposed identification preserves its explanandum, the thing to be explained, rather than replacing it. An identity earns its explanatory relief only once it is warranted, and the mere possibility of an identity explains nothing in particular. Neither move comes free, and the record counts the cost of both.

Illusionism and the acquaintance exchange

Illusionism makes the reductionist's burden, to preserve the explanandum, unusually explicit. It does so by refusing to preserve it, openly. On the inspected author version, Frankish keeps experience in an inclusive sense: perceptual activity, access, reports and introspective representation. What he rejects is genuinely qualitative properties, whether physical or nonphysical. The central task then becomes explaining why a system represents itself as possessing such properties [S-P2T05-001]. Two qualifications from the record go with this. The account describes itself as a programme with proposed content mechanisms, not as a completed explanation. And its motivations (conservative explanation, introspective fallibility, and abduction from anomalousness) are proposals whose premises are contested.

The programme's structural discipline deserves to be separated from its truth. It avoids the conscious spectator. No inner audience inspects the internal representations, because such an audience would recreate the problem one level up. It also meets the regress worry, which is that each apparent feel needs a higher representation, so that we face either a circle or a concealed phenomenal starting point. Its answer is fine-grained nonphenomenal seemings and a limited higher-order stop. The record's assessment is careful on this point. Once functional seeming and limited introspection are allowed, the regress identifies a real explanatory constraint, not an automatic contradiction. What the constraint costs is a worked account of why nonphenomenal representations present determinate apparent qualities. No such completed account is in the record.

The recorded objection is stronger than the slogan that an illusion must itself be conscious. Its author notes that quick line and sets it aside. The text of Nida-Rümelin's that was inspected is an author manuscript; its final print was not available for byte-level or visual verification. On that manuscript she identifies experiential properties through subjects and examples, so that their reference does not depend on any description an illusionist could reject. She then argues that to instantiate the relevant property is already to be aware of it [S-P2T05-002]. Acquaintance, on this account, is not a second introspective judgment that could misrepresent some independently occurring state. There is no gap in which the illusionist's error theory can live. Her conclusion is expressly conditional on an experiential example actually being instantiated.

Frankish's inspected reply grants the ordinary examples, and it grants the reformulation in terms of subjects and properties, for the sake of argument. What it denies is the one thing the objection needs: that reference by example fixes the property's realist nature [S-P2T05-003]. What the examples pick out may admit a representational interpretation. Cognitive access to structural physical properties requires mediation, so robust unmediated acquaintance is rejected. The reply candidly derives that rejection from physicalist commitments, and it expects not to persuade this critic. So the exchange ends with each side's decisive premise intact and openly identified: the adequacy of the functional account on one side, the identification of awareness with instantiation on the other.

The dispute, then, is about what must be preserved when the examples are explained, and this thesis keeps the article's framing of the stalemate. To define every seeming as phenomenal is to assume the realist premise. To define apparent character as nothing beyond representation is to assume too much the other way. The stronger illusionist response must explain the richness and authority of phenomenal judgments without quietly relying on their truth. The stronger realist response must show why its identification of the target resists that explanation. Within this dialectic, experiential primitiveness gives the realist a genuine dialectical advantage, and the record states it carefully: it preserves the target rather than revising it. What it does not yet establish is that preservation is explanatorily superior to a successful account of misrepresentation. Whether a misrepresentation account can succeed is the very thing the record marks unresolved.

Emergence, exclusion and latent powers

Accepting the target does not choose an ontology, and the causal-emergence exchange shows why. O'Connor's 1994 formulation required supervenience, nonstructurality and downward causal influence [S-P2T01-003]. The 2005 account written with Wong explicitly repudiates the supervenience requirement. In its place it posits basic nonstructural properties that organized physical systems causally generate and sustain over time [S-P2T01-001]. Both are inspected originals. The record treats the two dependence accounts as distinct, and they must not be merged. The model that matters downstream is the temporal one, on which the emergent is generated and maintained.

Kim's challenge is narrower than its reputation, and sharper for it. The text inspected is the 2008 reprint; the original 1999 journal pagination was not inspected. He allows ordinary downward causation, and he treats phenomenal qualia as promising candidates for irreducibility. His argument is this. Where an emergent has a sufficient noncausal basal condition, the base threatens to preempt the emergent as a cause, and moving the emergent's effects later in time does not by itself remove the threat [S-P2T01-002]. The argument rests on premises of its own: noncausal determination, basal sufficiency, and the exclusion of systematic overdetermination. The record marks these as substantive commitments, not logical truths.

The structural lesson of the reply is that naming an emergent property answers nothing. A premise has to be rejected. The 2005 account rejects the noncausal-dependence premise. If the base causally generates the emergent, then the emergent is an intermediate cause in a temporal process rather than a redundant competitor, and the preemption inference fails against this model. The escape has its costs, and the record lists them. There is a disputed simplicity comparison against latent-power alternatives. There is a thought experiment that stipulates its own discriminating conditions. The first-person motivation is contested. And there is no independently confirmed emergence law.

Shoemaker's inspected countercase supplies the other horn of a productive choice. His micro-latent powers are powers of ordinary microconstituents that are manifested only in suitable, emergence-engendering combinations. They let the complete microfacts fix the macrofacts, while still distinguishing causal power from mere nomic necessitation. His preferred treatment, in terms of identity and description, differs from irreducible emergent properties. He also considers a distinct-property option and rejects it on the strength of his commitments about essential individuation [S-P2T01-004]. The record's point of comparison is a demanding one. A successful reply to Kim does not certify the nonstructural ontology. To decide between the replies, one has to adjudicate property individuation, dependence and specified empirical discriminators. Counting replies settles nothing.

What the exchange establishes, then, is a choice of explanatory commitments rather than a winner. Reject the qualified completeness of a nonexperiential causal account, and irreducible experiential causation becomes intelligible. But that rejection carries an obligation to explain the actual causal organization, including the neural-dependence patterns that any such model inherits and must reproduce. Preserve completeness through latent powers instead, and the mirror obligation follows: one must specify powers that nothing in their familiar manifestations predicts. Neither side wins by treating its own causal vocabulary as compulsory. The record's inventory of costs is explicitly not a parsimony score and not a ranking.

Constitution, combination and individuation

Experiential constitution moves the difficulty a third time. The version inspected is the later authorial one. The exact 2006 journal text was never inspected, and the two are one work-version family, not independent corroboration. On that version Strawson argues conditionally. Experience is concrete. Assume that reality is physical and constituted by ultimates. No experiential reality can emerge from wholly nonexperiential reality. So some ultimates involve experience, and panpsychism proper requires a further homogeneity premise [S-P2T03-004]. The record treats the no-radical-emergence premise as a defended intuition, not a demonstration, and it treats the conclusion as conditional on premises it leaves unassessed.

Goff's inspected objection asks why the forbidden brute emergence does not simply reappear one level down. A plurality of experiences does not transparently constitute a further, unified experience. And if experiential being is exhaustively how it feels, then a constituent's character should show up in the character of the whole. Slight pains do not add up to a severe pain of a new quality [S-P2T03-002]. The objection also carries a charge of parity. Strawson uses experiential transparency against a posteriori physicalist identification, and that use threatens to prove too much, because experiential constitution is no more transparent than the physicalist's identity.

The inspected reply concedes the ground it can afford and keeps the claim it cannot do without. Strawson accepts that the objection refutes one full-revelation reading of his view. He distinguishes first-order acquaintance from exhaustive disclosure, so that the absence of microsubjects in acquaintance does not establish the absence of microsubjects. He revises his ontology toward active experiences with internal causal powers, and he acknowledges that as a cost. He acknowledges a defective argument in his own text at the recorded locus. And he concedes that he has no account of how microexperience constitutes macroexperience; the claim that it does rests on a transcendental argument plus smallism [S-P2T03-003]. The record's verdict is restraint in both directions. Constitution is not established, impossibility is not demonstrated, and the positive burden remains undischarged.

Taking experience as basic therefore answers the demand for experiential ingredients, but it transfers the burden to organization and ownership. Which pluralities compose? Under what relation? And why does the result have one subject rather than many, or none? The transfer is not a failure. As the alternatives chapter recorded, every family carries transferred burdens. But it is a debt, and the record forbids paying it with the observation that the ingredients are at least of the right kind. Having the right kind of ingredient is the definitional relief of this chapter's opening section, bought a second time at a lower level. It explains nothing about composition.

A universal consciousness reverses the direction of the same debt. Instead of composing one subject out of many, it must differentiate many private perspectives within one. The article's framing is that the reversal still owes an account of private perspectives and individuation. The legitimate attraction of a conscious ground, in either direction, is explanatory continuity: experience never has to arise from the nonexperiential. But continuity supplies no criterion of subject boundaries, no account of physical structure, and no discount on the individuation debt. Nor does the inspected constitution exchange lend the reversal any support. Micropsychism and experiential constitution entail no universal subject.

The transferred burdens of a conscious ground

What does primitiveness buy? This chapter answers with the article's two listed advantages. That answer is a chapter-level application of the article's inventory, not a census of the ledger, and it is conditional throughout on the demand being correctly identified. The first advantage is definitional relief. If the explanatory demand is correctly identified, it is met in the definite sense that nothing experiential has to arise from a stipulated nonexperiential base. The second is dialectical position. Against illusionism, the primitivist preserves the target rather than revising it, and that matters if and only if the target's realist identification survives the representational alternative. Both purchases are conditional on the demand being a fact about the world rather than a fact about our concepts. That is the very point Papineau's deflation contests, and the record leaves it standing as contested.

What primitiveness does not buy is any of the following. A recorded boundary blocks each one. It does not buy explanatory superiority, because preservation of the target has not been shown superior to a successful misrepresentation account; no completed account exists on either side. It does not buy a mechanism. Generation laws, constitution relations and individuation criteria are all recorded as unsupplied. It does not buy empirical advantage. The 2005 maintenance model must reproduce the neural-dependence patterns it inherits, and it must specify discriminating dynamics before any empirical comparison can begin. The constitution defender's recorded bound is his own acceptance of the relevant experiential states as brain states. And none of the inspected texts supplies a discriminating prediction. Finally, it does not buy actuality. That a model is conditionally available, whether causal-emergent, constitutive or universal, means only that it is available. It is no evidence that the model is instantiated.

The component boundaries translate these refusals into the thesis's working vocabulary. If the 2005 emergence ontology is granted, a limited property-level reading of conscious fundamentality can be satisfied despite instance-dependence. That is a conditional fact about the taxonomy, not an acceptance of the component. Causal mental influence under a maintenance condition establishes nothing about independence from brain function. Micropsychism and constitution entail no universal subject, no survival and no brain-independent consciousness, and the inspected constitution defender accepts the relevant experiential states as brain states. The inspected constitution chapter treats experience as requiring time, and the reply distinguishes activity from intentional agency. Neither statement supplies timelessness, preservation, intention, goodness or authority as established. Nothing else in these exchanges does either.

The dependence accounting for this chapter is short and strict. The gap debate and its phenomenal-concepts sequel are one dependent conceptual thread with shared authors: a single Chalmers-Papineau group, not two. The illusionism material is one exchange. The emergence material is one exchange. The constitution material is one exchange within one work-version family. That makes four dependency groups, no more and no fewer: DG-T05-epistemic-ontological-gap, DG-P2T05-Frankish-Nida-single-exchange, DG-P2T01-CAUSAL-EMERGENCE-EXCHANGE, and DG-P2T03-2006-symposium-and-2008-version. However many paragraphs this chapter spends, its evidential base is four conversations, and the cumulative chapter counts it that way.

Let me be clear about what this chapter has not shown. It cannot conclude that the explanatory demand is correctly identified. That is the standing dispute, and the record's deflationary attributions are as well documented as its antireductionist ones. It cannot conclude that any experiential-primitive ontology is actual, and it cannot rank the inspected causal-emergent and constitutive models against each other, or against the article's uninspected universal-consciousness reversal. It cannot turn the dialectical advantage of preserving the target into a probability of realism. All twelve withheld stronger-conclusion gates remain withheld through this chapter, and every assessment that uses this material downstream inherits every conditional stated here.

5From an Experiential Ground to an Intending Agent

The previous chapter asked what a theory buys, and what it owes, when it takes experience as basic. This chapter turns to a different question. By what routes might an experiential ground be argued to exist, and to be an agent? The record has two. Contingency arguments run from a principle of sufficient reason to a necessary foundation. Fine-tuning arguments run from life-permitting order to a designer's intentions. The question throughout comes from the article's fourth section: what such arguments can conditionally establish, where the bridge from a necessary foundation to a mind stays open, and what a discrimination argument must assume before its comparison does any work. The chapter closes with the circularity constraint on designer preferences, the component boundaries, a dependence accounting and a plain statement of what it cannot conclude.

The caution that governed the previous chapter governs this one too, with one addition. Every input is an inspected philosophical text or a reviewed project analysis. Every "argues," "derives" and "concedes" is a documented attribution to an identified version, and an attribution never establishes that a premise is true. The addition concerns versions. The historical texts are English modernizations. One member of an exchange is known only through its opponents' quotation of it. One manuscript's version identity is unauthenticated. And the fine-tuning literature is recorded as a single shared dependency group. Nothing in this chapter multiplies into independent lines of support, and the dependence structure is set out in detail at the end.

Contingency and the weak principle of sufficient reason

The demand the contingency route answers is the article's own: a foundational theory must also say why this concrete order exists at all. The record's historical anchor is Leibniz's 1714 Monadology, inspected in the Bennett English modernization at the relevant sections. The original French was not compared. There Leibniz states a principle of sufficient reason for true or existent facts, and he distinguishes necessary truths from contingent ones. He then argues that even an infinite sequence of contingencies does not contain its own ultimate reason, and he concludes to a necessary substance [S-T10-002]. The record keeps a further step separate. Unity, perfection, understanding and will are attributed to the foundation in a distinct move, and calling a foundation necessary establishes none of them.

The record's historical counterweight is Hume's Dialogues, inspected in the Bennett English modernization at its relevant sections, with the editorial additions marked. It is read with speaker discipline, because no speaker uniformly expresses Hume's settled view. In Part IX Cleanthes objects to Demea's necessity argument through conceivability, through the possibility of a necessary material universe, and through the explanation of the parts. Philo adds an analogy with hidden necessity [S-T10-003]. Elsewhere Philo challenges the extrapolation from human artifacts and asks what a mental plan explains, and Cleanthes replies that explaining a cause does not require explaining its cause. These are positions in a dialogue, and they have contestable premises of their own. The record marks the conceivability premise and the conception of causal priority as open.

The project's reconstruction of the classical route is explicitly conditional. Its premises are a restricted principle of sufficient reason covering the contingent totality, a noncircular requirement of external explanation, and a concrete-ground premise. Together they conditionally yield a necessary concrete reality. Mentality needs further premises. The record marks each step as substantive, and it keeps the recorded objection and the recorded reply in their roles. The objection is that local explanations may exhaust the legitimate demand, and that a necessary impersonal reality remains possible. The reply is that local causal links can leave unanswered why the totality obtains, and that exemptions from the principle need justification. Meanwhile the scope of the principle itself stays a recorded unresolved issue, not a settled symmetric burden. Throughout, the contingency of the totality is not inferred merely from the contingency of its parts, and modal necessity is not temporal or causal priority. The alternatives also remain live throughout: a brute totality, a necessary impersonal ground, and rejection of the concrete-ground premise.

The developed modern version in the record is the Gale–Pruss argument, inspected in the full selected text of the 1999 article. Its advertised novelty is modal weakness. Instead of asserting that the totality of contingent truths has an explanation, it assumes only that it could have one. That is a weak principle of sufficient reason, ranging over propositions true in any possible world. The argument combines that principle with a maximal world-individuating contingent conjunction, bivalence and compossibility, and derives an actual explanation [S-P2T10-001]. The record separates the formal statement from its informal gloss. The formal scope is broader than a reading confined to the actual world, and the two must not be interchanged. The attraction, as the article frames it and this chapter keeps it, is this: explain contingency through something that does not merely add another unexplained contingent member.

Oppy's criticism attacks the advertised modesty. It was inspected in the selected manuscript text of the 2000 reply; the print pagination was not collated. Under stated assumptions about explanation, namely veridicality and dissectivity within normal modal reasoning, the weak principle entails the strong one. The route is Fitch-style, and it does not depend on the maximal-world construction [S-P2T10-002]. The record retains his care with the logic. Dissectivity is not agglomerativity, and an explanation existing for a conjunction is not the conjunction of two explanations explaining the joint obtaining. The epistemic point rides on the logical one. A person who assented to the weak wording without grasping its consequences may rationally withdraw that assent once the entailment is made explicit, provided they have independent grounds for beliefs that conflict with the conclusion. The record notes that this proviso is a condition of the argument, not a demonstrated fact about all non-theists.

Two further distinctions of Oppy's are recorded, because the downstream assessment uses them. First, an explained counterpart or physical duplicate of the universe is not the same object as the actual world-complete fact. So the intuitive possibility of the former does not by itself support the weak principle over the latter. Second, questions about abstract propositions, and about the difference between the big conjunctive fact and its contingent restriction, are flagged but severable. The record notes Oppy's own statement that his main criticism does not depend on resolving them, and the ontology of the totality remains a separate open issue.

The Gale–Pruss reply of 2002, inspected in the full selected version, concedes the entailment and moves the defense elsewhere. The weak and strong principles are reformulated for contingent propositions, and the deduction is accepted. The reply then argues as follows. Understanding a proposition does not require grasping all of its consequences. The practice of seeking explanations gives the weak principle epistemic appeal. And a restricted totality fallback is available, conditionally, against Davey and Clifton [S-P2T10-003]. Version limits attach to that last point. Davey and Clifton are known here only as the reply itself presents them; their original was not inspected. The Pruss 2001 cardinality proof that stands behind the construction was also uninspected. Modern sufficient-reason treatments beyond this exchange were not inspected either. The record's sharpest annotation concerns a bridge inside the defense. The reply moves from epistemic possibility to logical possibility, treating that as a reasonable step after adequate inquiry. That modal warrant is defeasible and contested. It is not a deduction from ignorance.

The article's verdict on this exchange, which this thesis keeps, is that the reply blocks an easy accusation of unintelligibility but does not make the disclosed consequences costless. The question becomes whether commitment to the explanatory principle, understood at full modal strength, remains better justified than its rivals. That is a comparison of intuitions and practices, not a further theorem. In the record as inspected, the cosmological argument provides a structured challenge to brute contingency. It does not provide an uncontested passage from intelligibility to providence.

From necessary foundation to mind: the open bridge

Suppose we grant an actual explanation of the totality. The step to an agent is still a separate argument, with pressure points of its own on the record. The Gale–Pruss route treats personal and scientific explanation as exhaustive, a premise their own text qualifies by conceding that unconceived kinds of explanation may exist. It excludes scientific explanation, because contingent laws and states fall inside the totality and cannot explain themselves. It distinguishes necessary existence from contingent creative action, to avoid modal collapse. And it ends the regress in a necessary being's libertarian free action, presented as self-explaining, with fullness of explanation relative to the questioner's context. The record sets a cost against each move. The exhaustiveness premise is not a measured inventory. The free-action exception is a substantive explanatory premise. And further reasons can still be requested.

The recorded impersonal rival enters through the authors themselves. Gale and Pruss report Quinn's objection that a necessary impersonal source could produce the cosmos by blind indeterministic causation. Only their published quotation of his correspondence was inspected; no independent Quinn original was. Their response allows a generic necessary powerful cause as a fallback, while defending personality by appeal to cosmic order and to a distinction between free action and merely indeterministic action. The project's strongest recorded question sits right here. Why is libertarian self-explanation sufficient where impersonal indeterminism is not, once explanatory fullness has been made relative to context? The record leaves that question open on both sides.

The attribute stage is recorded as candidly incomplete in the original texts. The 1999 argument adds teleological premises for the creator's qualities, but it explicitly withholds the omni-properties, and it acknowledges that actual benevolence needs theodicy and faith. A footnote records the two authors disagreeing between themselves about whether the possibility of gratuitous evil defeats an essentially perfect necessary God. The 2002 reply preserves the same gap from the other direction. Evidence against benevolence does not by itself exclude every necessary creator, since an explanation of a horrendous universe could fall short of God. Nothing in the exchange turns a necessary source into a good one.

The third voice on the bridge in the record is Lennox, inspected as a full essay in HTML. The venue is apologetic, and the record reads the essay's quoted historical statements and its resurrection case as context, not as corroborated events. The Ford analogy makes mechanism and agent compatible: explaining how a process operates does not exclude an agent explanation of why it was arranged [S-T10-001]. The essay then advances positive claims of its own, separately. It offers discoverable intelligibility and sophisticated lawlike order as reasons for a rational creator, and it challenges naturalism's warrant for reason. It also treats natural laws as regularities that do not, on their own, rule out supernatural agency. Direct critics of the essay were not inspected.

The record's assessment keeps the two steps strictly apart. Compatibility removes one alleged incompatibility, and by itself it favors nothing. Comparative support is a different matter. It requires independently motivated expectations under which the observed order is more expected on a rational origin than on a defined impersonal alternative. The recorded objections hold both conditions open. Agent preferences and the contrast class may be fitted after the world has been observed. Impersonal structure and selection-plus-learning alternatives are not empirically tested here. And no equation of nonintentional origins with unreliable reason is validated. The order appeals also share assumptions with the attribute stage of the cosmological argument, so putting the two together adds no independent sample.

The open bridge can now be stated in the record's own terms. A necessary explanatory source does not by itself establish a conscious bearer, unity, bodily independence, personal survival, timelessness, preserving purpose or moral authority. Each component needs its own bridge, and the absence of a proven rival does not prove its negation. The article sharpens the point about method: personal explanation cannot be privileged solely because the intended conclusion is personal. What the contingency route delivers, at the reconstruction's conditional maximum, is a necessary concrete ground. Everything this thesis cares about lies on the far side of that result.

Fine-tuning, stringency and screening

Fine-tuning promises a more discriminating route. Its claim is not that something necessary exists, but that the specific life-permitting character of the order favors an agent with discernible preferences. The record carries Collins in two versions, and the difference between them matters. The abridged teaching excerpt of the 1999 chapter was inspected as a partial original with ellipses. It lacks the probability appendix it refers to, and a printed formula in it is missing a division, which the record preserves as a reproduction issue [S-T10-004]. The 1998 webpage was inspected in its sections, appendices and endnotes, and it does contain a probability appendix, restricted to an illuminated range with a defended parameterization. But it is not an authenticated complete copy of the 1999 chapter, and its second endnote repeats a malformed likelihood factor. The corrected standard identity of that factor is the project's own labeled reconstruction [S-P2T11-003]. On both versions the argument is explicitly comparative and short of proof. It deals in epistemic probability. It claims that a life-permitting range is less expected under an atheistic single universe. And it offers no all-things-considered verdict.

The design side of the comparison is carried by the designer's motivations, and the record states the cost of that dependence before it uses it. Collins motivates life under theism through divine goodness and the value of intelligent conscious beings. Goodness and the value of life are therefore premises of the likelihood, and the record forbids counting them a second time as independently established conclusions. The article states the general constraint this chapter ends on. The dependence is not inherently circular, since a separately constrained conception of agency could predict life. But it becomes circular if life is the sole ground for attributing the very preference that is then invoked to explain life.

The stringency debate sharpens the evidential question to its cleanest recorded form. White, inspected in the published four-page 2011 article, separates old knowledge from new. The old knowledge is that life exists and that the universe is hospitable. The new knowledge is that the conditions for life are stringent [S-P2T11-001]. He argues from three premises: that without design, life depends negatively on stringency; that given design, the two are independent; and that stringency did not count against design beforehand. From these he derives that newly learned stringency supports design even when existence is already known. He interprets the probabilities evidentially, so the absence of repeated universe creation is not by itself a refutation. He states limits of his own, and the record keeps them. The third premise is motivated by the absence of a known designer preference, and the conclusion is bounded by other fine-tuning objections. One version boundary matters here. White's stated target is a 2010 Weisberg article that was not inspected. The Weisberg text this record does inspect is the 2012 manuscript discussed in the next paragraph.

Weisberg's critique targets that rationale for the third premise [S-T10-007]. It was inspected in the seven-page author manuscript of the 2012 article rather than in the journal pagination. The Bradley reply is known only through that manuscript and remains uninspected, while White's article is inspected separately, as above. His judge example shows that the inference from no known preference to no negative evidential relevance can fail. His central result derives screening-off from matched divine and blind conditional distributions over life-permitting possibilities. Once life is known, stringency adds nothing, under those matching assumptions. His Divine Intent assumption also supports an unconditional stringency-negative derivation in his larger argument. The record keeps the result conditional in both directions. It is valid under the matching distributions, and it is not a measure-free refutation of all design arguments. Weisberg himself allows the initial evidence of life to support design, while acknowledging problems of uniformity, unbounded space, parameterization and method selection.

The recorded extension is Dorst and Dorst, inspected as the complete ten-page 2022 Online First PDF [S-P2T11-002]. That version has visible defects in its formulas and bibliography, which required explicit project reconstruction; one is a final inequality sign that conflicts with an earlier equality. They recast White's premise as indifference over stringency classes rather than over life-worlds, and the two uniformities then yield opposite conditional support relations. Their setup also makes laxity disfavor design after conditioning on life, in just the place where stringency favors it, while the unconditional relation can differ. They then offer a mixture argument. If some possible ideal distribution over stringency classes makes the observed stringency more expected under design, and reasonable credence gives that possibility sufficient weight, then conditional positive support survives uncertainty. It also survives those departures from exact indifference that preserve the likelihood separation. The record's annotations are strict. The tiny no-design likelihood at the bound is assumed from a source, Collins 2003, which is a distinct source not inspected here and not either of the two Collins versions above. The diagrams are stipulated spaces rather than measured frequencies. And the weighting depends on disputed views of the designer, of time and of laws.

The recorded counterweight is a supplied manuscript that its archive attributes to McCutcheon [S-P2T11-005]. The supplied bytes carry no author byline and no version tag, so the claimed version identity is unauthenticated. The manuscript introduces uncertainty over the laxity range parameter itself. Its uniform-range-prior example collapses the enormous fixed-range ratio to about sixty-one. That figure is a small-interval approximation, and its extreme input value is assumed from the exchange, not empirically validated. The example still explicitly allows residual positive stringency evidence. Its power priors approach a limiting ratio near unity, while acknowledging that the exact endpoint prior is improper. Further limits on the model are also recorded. It assumes linear life-world weighting and a particular hyperprior, and its count-versus-proportion and maximum-versus-typical interpretations of the range are not independently established. The record keeps both directions conditional. The critique challenges the scale and robustness of the mixture advantage without invalidating the mixture's sufficient condition. Its own near-neutral conclusion is conditional on the model assumptions it adds. And a capped forward search found no actual reply; absence of discovery, the record notes, is not absence of reply.

Sober supplies the recorded observation-selection critique, and the history of access to it is part of the record. The earlier task inspected only the abstract, notes and references of the publisher preview [S-T10-006]. The later task read the full published scan, pages 63 to 90. His analysis distinguishes qualitative favoring from numerical strength, and it grants the fine-tuning inequality for the sake of argument. The critique conditions the observation of stable life-compatible constants on the earlier existence of observers, and that is not automatically the same datum as newly learned stringency. A self-revision is also documented in the record. He retracts his 2004 firing-squad treatment after Weisberg 2005, while keeping a different judgment for fine-tuning; both of the older originals are uninspected. He also declines to debunk every design argument, and the record notes that he does not claim the design probabilities actually equal zero.

What does the stringency exchange establish? Conditional model behavior, and nothing stronger; the record refuses every stronger reading. Fine-tuning can supply conditional qualitative comparative support without a numerical posterior. Sensitivity alone identifies neither a measure nor a designer's preferences. Matched conditional distributions neutralize stringency where their matching assumptions hold, and nowhere else. And none of the inspected arguments establishes a physical measure or identifies brute reality with a uniform random sampler. The article makes the same point in a way this chapter keeps. Put a life-valuing preference on one side and an invented uniform distribution on the other, and an apparently enormous explanatory gain appears. That gain is an artifact of those inputs, since neither unspecified brute reality nor unspecified agency has that distribution by definition.

Conditional discrimination and its assumptions

The article's conclusion for this material, which this chapter keeps as its framing, is conditional discrimination. Observer selection does not trivially erase every relevant difference, and the preceding paragraphs have said which differences it leaves standing. And the unavailability of numerical probabilities does not mean that every qualitative comparison is empty. But a useful design argument must identify the evidence being updated on, the alternatives in play, and the assumptions that govern their expectations. A conscious ground earns additional support from design only through a defensible account of what it would be inclined to produce. The circularity constraint gives that requirement teeth. Preferences attributed solely from the datum they are invoked to explain confer no support at all.

The component boundaries translate the limits of the two routes into the thesis's working vocabulary. The inspected existence and design routes do not by themselves establish universal subjecthood, human brain independence, survival, timelessness, preservation, goodness or moral authority. Life-directed preferences that are used to support design cannot then independently establish goodness, preservation or moral authority. That prohibition on circular credit is a recorded inference, not a stylistic caution. And the recorded conclusion about the selected versions is that they leave all twelve stronger-conclusion gates withheld. This chapter opens none of them.

The dependence accounting for this chapter is layered, because the sources are. The modern cosmological exchange (the 1999 argument, the 2000 criticism and the 2002 reply) is one dependency group, DG-P2T10-PSR-EXCHANGE. The historical Leibniz and Hume claims are recorded separately. They carry their own T10 dependency groups, and the ledger's statement that the v1 Hume and Leibniz claims remain derivative historical inputs is itself a distinct group, DG-P2T10-V1-INFERENCE. The order-and-intelligibility material, DG-P2T10-LENNOX-ORDER, shares assumptions with the attribute stage of the cosmological argument, so putting the two together supplies no independent sample count. The entire fine-tuning corpus is recorded under a single shared dependency group, DEP-P2T11-FINETUNING-SHARED-LIFE-MEASURE-PREFERENCES. That corpus is Collins in both versions, White, Weisberg, Dorst and Dorst, the supplied critique and Sober. The standing instruction for cumulative use is recorded with it: retain the shared order, sufficient-reason and agent-preference dependencies, and do not multiply them as independent observations.

Let me be clear about what this chapter has not shown. It cannot conclude that the totality of contingent facts has an actual explanation. That conclusion is conditional on a principle whose full modal strength is the very thing the recorded exchange contests. It cannot conclude that any necessary foundation exists, that any foundation is personal, or that fine-tuning confers any all-things-considered support on design. The fine-tuning support claims in this chapter are conditional likelihood comparisons whose measures, preferences and priors remain unestablished. The sufficient-reason and intelligibility routes are different in kind: one is a conditional deduction from a restricted principle and the other a conditional abduction, each still contested, and neither turns on a measure or a prior. It cannot use the goodness assumed inside the design likelihood as evidence of goodness. All twelve withheld stronger-conclusion gates remain withheld through this chapter, and every assessment that uses this material downstream inherits every conditional stated here.

6Neural Dependence and the Evidential Role of Crisis Experiences

The two chapters before this one weighed the philosophical routes to a conscious foundation. We now turn to the empirical material, taken over from the article's fifth section. Two questions organize it. What do controlled neural interventions establish about the dependence of experience on brain activity? And what can crisis-experience reports, the near-death and resuscitation material, show and not show? Everything in this chapter turns on one standard, and it is a conjunctive one. A crisis report discriminates between rival accounts of mind only when three things are established together: externally accurate content, a defensible interval of acquisition, and adequate information about the processes available during that interval. Four case dossiers, three prospective studies, a historical and cross-cultural corpus, and their chains of critic and reply are all measured against that standard. The chapter closes with the evidential asymmetry between the two streams, the dependence accounting, and a plain statement of what none of this material concludes.

The caution that governed the previous chapters governs this one too, with some additions that empirical material requires. Every clinical number, timing estimate and description of experience in this chapter is an attribution to an identified inspected version. None of it is a measurement this project verified. Two of the case dossiers rest partly on user-supplied purported transcripts whose audio, speaker identity and timestamps were never verified. Two rest partly on converted ebooks whose conversion metadata postdates publication. Several rest on current webpages that are not authenticated snapshots of their original wording. The distinction to hold onto is simple: a verified report is not a verified event. Each narrator's crisis and recovery episodes form one reporting dependency group, so retellings, translations and republications never add observations. Counts keep their units throughout. People, resuscitation episodes, interviews, EEG images and analysis windows are not interchangeable. The dependency structure itself is set out in the closing section.

Controlled interventions and their scope

The article asks for discrimination, not mere covariation, and the cleanest intervention case in the record supplies it, at the scale of a single patient. In Parvizi and colleagues' 2012 study, electrical stimulation of a right fusiform face-selective region pair in one 45-year-old epilepsy patient produced reported face distortions on seven real-face trials. Four sham trials and three trials stimulating a nearby control pair produced none [S-T09-004]. That is more than correlation gives us. The sham and control contrasts support a causal contribution within this case. The limits deserve equal care, and the ledger states them. Nonspecific nonface changes also occurred. The two stimulated regions were not separated, and current can affect connected sites. The small famous-face and place naming tests differed in procedure, so their null differences support no conclusion of equivalence. Randomization and assessor masking were not established. A 2013 correction changes only an author name [S-T09-005]. One patient's repeated trials are not independent people, and no unitary face center follows from them. The question of local versus network remains open.

Tasserie and colleagues' 2022 macaque study carries the intervention evidence from content to state. Under maintained propofol anesthesia, high central-thalamic deep brain stimulation raised the reported behavioral arousal score from 0 of 11 to 9 of 11 in both stimulated animals. Low central-thalamic stimulation reached 3 of 11, and ventrolateral control stimulation stayed at 0 of 11; awake animals score 11 of 11 [S-T09-002]. Stimulation also shifted fMRI dynamics and enhanced hierarchical auditory responses. The qualifications here are structural rather than incidental. The intervention unit is two animals, with three separate awake comparators and reused awake auditory data. Blinding and a randomized order of conditions were not established in the inspected text. Subjective awareness is inferred from signatures, since macaques give no trialwise reports. The auditory paradigm had no ventrolateral control arm. And the supplementary methods disclose that each animal's high stimulation setting was chosen empirically, just above its own behavioral arousal threshold, which limits any reading of the result as an unselected dose-response estimate [S-P2T19-002]. One more caution about units. Per-run volume counts and overlapping analysis windows are not run totals by animal and condition, and those totals were not located in the inspected extents.

Does an unresponsive patient have no experience? Scheinin and colleagues' anesthesia-and-sleep study, published in 2021, is the record's sharpest warning against assuming so. Forty healthy young men were recruited. Of these, 39 underwent randomized open-label propofol (19) or dexmedetomidine (20) anesthesia, and 37 returned for a sleep arm on average eighteen weeks later. These are overlapping arms, not 76 participants [S-T09-003]. Among interviewable states, subjective experience was reported in 80% of anesthesia interviews and 71% of sleep interviews, including contentless impressions. Two cautions apply. These are interview rates, not population prevalence, and the exact integer numerators and denominators behind the percentages are not supplied in the inspected main text. A targeted Phase 2 search of the study's extended-data appendices found regional PET cluster tables rather than interview-count tables, so the integers remain unrecovered. The paper's PET contrasts locate state-linked changes in thalamic, cingulate and angular blood flow. Those are associations within manipulated states, not tests of focal necessity. And the authors themselves expressly note that absent response, or absent recalled content, cannot conclusively establish absent experience.

The Cogitate adversarial collaboration, whose main results appeared in Nature in 2025, is the largest theory-level test in the record. The consortium recruited 256 participants across fMRI (120), MEG (102) and intracranial EEG (34), and retained 108, 97 and 32. The main noninvasive tests used 73 held-out fMRI and 65 held-out MEG datasets, while the invasive data were not split [S-T09-001]. Suprathreshold visual stimuli in four categories at three durations were tested under varying task relevance. The design did not directly contrast conscious with unconscious processing, and it did not perturb candidate regions. The operational predictions set posterior against prefrontal decoding, posterior duration tracking against prefrontal onset/offset ignition, and posterior against long-range prefrontal synchrony. The outcome is mixed. Sustained posterior representations were partly supported, and transient prefrontal category decoding appeared. But the predicted prefrontal offset profile and sustained posterior gamma synchrony were not established, and the preregistered pairwise-phase-consistency analysis supported neither critical connectivity prediction [S-T09-008]. An exploratory amplitude-based dynamic functional connectivity analysis partly supported the workspace theory's expectation, while still lacking sustained posterior connectivity. Exploratory it remains; it cannot be relabeled a preregistered confirmation. No overall winner among the theories follows.

The Phase 2 protocol audit qualifies the confirmatory weight of those results. It does not erase them. The selected OSF amendment file is named v4 but internally identified as Version 4.5 and dated 22 December 2022.
