The recorded inference from this overlap runs both ways, and this chapter takes it over whole. Phenomenological overlap after DMT shows that similar scale endorsements can arise in a drug context. It does not establish endogenous DMT as the cause of any particular crisis experience, and it does not explain away any specific anomalous detail. Conversely, the survival-friendly reply, that drugs might change access to experience rather than generate it, is a possible interpretation. It requires additional discriminating predictions; it is not a rebuttal that the data supply. Resemblance alone identifies neither mechanism nor ontology. The recorded recommendation is for prospective designs with independently scored information and resolved timing, together with the separate question of what identity relation any result would support.

The crisis-report material already audited contributes one thing here, and only one: reported temporal phenomenology as a documented thing to be explained. Alexander's reviewed account of altered duration, in which months or years seemed to pass, concerns remembered phenomenology. Its attribution is carried over from the talk transcript, whose audio was not verified. And it is not the same claim as a claim of no intrinsic succession [S-T06-001]. Reports from resuscitated subjects and aggregate brain-monitoring records do not supply the survival component's irreversible-death boundary, episode-specific experience timing or a numerical-identity bridge [S-T07-007]. Subjective temporal alteration is a finding to explain. It is not evidence of ontological timelessness, whether in the experiencer or in the world.

The coding discipline closes the loop between phenomenology and ontology. Reports of no succession, of simultaneous content, of indefinite duration and of later narrative order must be coded separately. Why? Because testimony may support the occurrence of an experience without establishing the ontology attributed to it. The converse guard is kept as well: an atemporal awareness could be narrated sequentially afterward, so narrative order on its own does not refute it [S-T08-006; S-T13-004]. No validated instrument in the record uniquely identifies timelessness. What we have to explain are documented and recurrent reports. Their ontological interpretation is just as open as the eternity exchange left it.

Dependence accounting, access gaps and non-conclusions

The dependence structure of this chapter is dominated by shared philosophical premises rather than by shared samples. The physics material falls into the Einstein kinematic formalism, the Rovelli relational formalism and the physics-to-metaphysics bridge. The classical and modern eternity material falls into the Boethian timeless-knowledge group, the Craig tensed-knowledge and personhood groups, the timeless-foundation construction, and the eternity-personhood exchange (Stump–Kretzmann 1981 and 1992, Lewis 1984 and 1988). The Lewis 1986–Oaklander 1987–88 exchange is a group of its own. The recorded shared-premises claim marks the Boethius and Craig discussions as non-independent. The identity criteria carry per-author groups (Locke, Olson, Swinburne), plus the project's identity-bridge inferences. The empirical material carries the DMT study group, the IANDS-descended retrospective group for the Greyson instrument, and the Alexander and resuscitation-report chains carried over from chapter 6, which add no new observations here. Nothing in this chapter sums across a group.

The access gaps are declared. The Craig essays lack publication dates in their inspected versions, and repository metadata attributing one of them to April 2000 was not independently checked. The modern defenders Craig criticizes, the authors Olson reconstructs, the accounts Oaklander cites, and the technical literatures behind the third-party discussions in the 1992 defense all remain uninspected originals. Parfit was not directly accessed. The published version of Rovelli's preprint, the original German Einstein, the Latin Boethius, the diplomatic Locke and the published 2003 Olson text were not inspected, and the Swinburne essay is not his book-length argument. A bounded forward search located no later Lewis rebuttal to Oaklander; that is a search result, not a fact about the literature. By contrast, the absence of any citation of Lewis 1984 from the complete body and notes of the 1992 paper is an inspection finding in the exact 1992 copy, not a search result. Let me be clear about what this chapter has not shown. It cannot conclude that any timeless model is actual, or that any is impossible. It cannot conclude that any identity criterion is correct. It cannot conclude that anyone survives death, or that any reported temporal phenomenology reveals the world's temporal ontology. The eternity proposal remains an improved but still unproven relation. The preservation substitution fails as an answer to survival. And every component of the fundamental-consciousness package (fundamentality, universal subjecthood, brain independence, survival beyond irreversible death, timelessness, preservation, intention, goodness, moral authority) remains just as unestablished after this chapter as it was before it. All twelve withheld stronger-conclusion gates remain withheld.

8Does Significance Require Endurance?

The previous chapter separated survival from preservation and weighed the cost of the timeless proposals that promise to avoid loss altogether. This chapter asks the value question that those proposals serve. Does significance require endurance? To answer it, the chapter expands the first half of the article's seventh section through two packets, the meaning-and-endurance audit and its Phase 2 reinspection. They cover the final-loss argument in its strongest inspected forms, Wolf's engagement account of finite meaning, the Williams–Fischer exchange over immortality, and Metz's purpose theory with its own author's qualifications. The chapter also develops the consciousness-factory proposal from the research plan. What would distinguish a universe that produces experience from one that exists in order to produce it, and what would a discriminating test require? The conclusion is taken over from the article: neither position licenses a blanket verdict. Finite meaning and valuable endurance are compatible claims. The necessity thesis lacks a defended bridge in the inspected exchanges. And conditional worthwhile continuation remains open.

The standing caution applies, together with this chapter's particular version limits. Every position is attributed to an identified inspected version, and the versions are these. The Craig essay is a current author-site version, with no publication date displayed in the inspected text. The Wolf material was inspected twice: as an institutionally hosted 2007–2008 lecture draft in the first audit, and as the final 2010 first lecture in the Phase 2 reinspection, with no claim that the two versions are textually identical. The Fischer article was inspected in a later reprint layout whose exact edition remains unresolved. The Williams chapter is a complete user-supplied Cambridge copy of the 1973 original. Metz's 2013 chapter is identity-verified but body-unavailable, so its position reaches this chapter only through his critics and through his own later reply. Metz 2000 and Yoshizawa 2015 were inspected as complete or near-complete originals. Accurate attribution is not the same as premise truth. Every substantive claim in this chapter carries the ledger's contested inference strength, and nothing here establishes any metaphysical component.

Loss, erasure and the final-loss argument

The article frames the strongest argument for endurance as one that begins with loss, not with vanity. Love, inquiry and creative activity can be worthwhile and yet vulnerable. Death removes the opportunity to sustain them, and it may frustrate projects whose value depends on their continuing. A final-loss argument can therefore claim that lasting preservation would improve a life, without assuming that only infinite achievements matter. The argument divides at its next step: from the badness of ending a valuable activity to the absence of value in the activity that occurred. Consider a friendship. If its ending is bad because the friendship mattered, that explanation does not by itself erase the friendship's earlier significance. The badness of the ending presupposes the value it ends; it does not cancel it.

The necessity position is documented in its strongest inspected advocate. Craig requires both God and immortality for ultimate significance, while denying that mere endless duration is enough. He presents resurrection and fellowship with God in positive terms, and he explicitly says that the discussion has not shown Christianity to be true [S-T14-001]. Both the position and its self-imposed limit are kept. This is a documented philosophical claim about what significance requires; it is not an established fact about value. The essay's psychological assertions and its claims against atheist morality remain unverified, and they lie outside the accepted attribution. The inspected version is the current author-site text. An older 1994 chapter identity is a lead from search metadata, nothing more.

The audit's reconstruction identifies what the necessity thesis owes. Endurance can enhance significance under stated premises: the same subjects are retained, the relevant goods are preserved, and additional worthwhile experience is available without overriding harm. But necessity requires more. It requires a further principle, that the loss of all enduring bearers or of all recognition defeats every relevant kind of significance, and not merely future continuation. That principle remains disputed. The recorded annotation is pointed: calling the target "ultimate" cannot settle the dispute about value by stipulation. Comparative enhancement and strict necessity are different theses, and they carry different burdens.

The Phase 2 reinspection supplies the exchange that the first audit lacked. Yoshizawa's 2015 study explicitly targets the seventh chapter of Metz's 2013 book. That chapter's identity is verified, but its body remains unavailable to this project, so its position is triangulated and never quoted [S-P2T13-002; S-P2T13-001]. Yoshizawa argues that a concern about losing an ordinary life that is already valuable is not captured merely by demanding perfect value. He builds a framework that separates the person who possesses meaning-making properties from the life being assessed, the whole from the part, and pro tanto meaning from on-balance meaning. His own earlier 2011 Japanese paper principally addressed Metz 2003. It was not inspected. The reconstruction of Tolstoy that he works from is, by his own footnote, expressly not certified as Tolstoy's intended argument.

Yoshizawa's decisive distinctions are the ones the erasure inference needs and does not have. He distinguishes an existing person who lacks meaning-making properties from there being no such person at all, and he refuses to infer general meaninglessness from the latter. In the end he rejects the general claim that a person's death itself makes her life meaningless, and he favors naturalistic objectivism. That is an attribution; it does not establish naturalism about consciousness. He allows that particular deaths prevent achievements, and that continuity, posthumous events and the survival of others can matter [S-P2T13-002]. He also expressly distinguishes the four-dimensional existence of a finite life from eternity or from transcending temporality. The record keeps that distinction with two limits: his quotation of Singer is not an independent inspection of Singer, and neither eternalism nor a timeless conscious bearer follows from it. These are attributed philosophical positions, contested at every joint. Their value is that they make the final-loss argument state its bridge instead of assuming it.

Metz's actual 2015 reply, inspected in its relevant sections, constrains the exchange in both directions. He grants the distinction between the absence of perfect value and the loss of imperfect value, and he grants that the latter deserves attention. This concedes that a rationale was omitted; it does not concede that the rationale is sound [S-T14-004]. He then objects that to call the ended life already meaningful is to concede meaning without immortality, and he proposes a stricter rationale under which the ending is what makes the life meaningless. He further asks whether love or contemplation, endlessly retained, would still be merely ordinary value. His suggested response on Yoshizawa's behalf is marked in the record as hypothetical, since no inspected later publication continues the chain. Neither formulation proves a finite life valueless.

The recorded adjudication of the final-loss exchange is a finding of failure to establish, with explicit limits. The selected exchange does not establish that ending a life erases its earlier value. To treat absence as an existing state that lacks meaning requires a further bridge, and no inspected author supplies it. There is, of course, room for the strongest advocate of strict necessity to dispute the retrospective attribution of value, and the packet settles no theory of value or of time. But the unmet burden is the bridge from loss to meaninglessness, and the reconstructed strict rationale is endorsed by neither of the authors who examine it. The missing 2013 original remains the exchange's declared access gap. No later summary substitutes for it.

Finite meaningful engagement

Wolf's account is the article's developed alternative to erasure, and the record supports its use within stated limits. Meaning, on her account, arises through active, caring engagement with objects that are independently worthy; subjective attraction and objective worth constrain each other. Pleasure alone is not enough, and neither is beneficial activity wholly disconnected from engagement [S-T14-002]. Her illustrative counterexamples are philosophical devices, not clinical findings. The strength of the account, as the article puts it, is that it identifies a kind of significance for which endless duration is no part of the proposed explanation. A finite episode could matter to the person living it, and be worth that concern, without any claim about what happens later.

Wolf's own limits are kept, because they block a substitution this chapter must not make. She acknowledges that her account need not address the desire for a large and lasting cosmic impact. Her final first lecture explicitly limits what a qualitative account can offer a person distressed by cosmic smallness, and she declines to promise either a theory of objective value or an infallible procedure for identifying meaning [S-T14-002]. These are findings from the final 2010 lecture. The Phase 2 reinspection recorded them under the same source identifier, whose canonical provenance remains the 2007–2008 draft, and no claim is made that the two versions are textually identical. Meaning within a life cannot simply replace the enduring or cosmic target that motivates the survival literature. The record states this as a scope limit on both sides. The second lecture, the commentaries and the response were not inspected.

The finite-value argument that the audit builds on this base is conditional, and it says so. A finite instance of learning, care or relief can benefit actual subjects, and the worth of that benefit need not depend entirely on later recognition. The non-erasure principle says that later absence, on its own, does not retroactively make every earlier benefit worthless. That principle grounds finite significance under an explicit value premise. It does not answer why a cosmos exists or whether its subjects endure. The recorded objection and reply are both kept. The objection is that a meaningful episode may still lack an ultimately worthwhile setting. The reply distinguishes missing further goods from the nonexistence of prior goods, and it expressly does not claim to answer the larger question of purpose.

The desirability of endurance

Williams's challenge, inspected in the complete user-supplied 1973 chapter, is stronger than the boredom caricature, and the record keeps it that way. He grounds our reasons to avoid death in categorical desires, the desires that motivate us to go on living. Death can therefore be bad at a particular time even if endless life is undesirable, and it does not follow that every additional period of life is harmful [S-T14-007]. His challenge to immortality then imposes two conditions. An adequate immortal prospect must preserve the person, and it must relate appropriately to the aims that motivate survival. Psychologically disjoint survival can meet the first condition while failing the second. Being the same person and having a future worth anticipating as one's own are different requirements.

The heart of the challenge is impoverishment, not unpleasantness. Williams argues that enduring human life threatens categorical motivation through an impoverished relation to the world, and not merely through an unpleasant feeling of boredom. An extended life of fixed character undermines motivation, while disjoint or radically accumulated lives fail the condition of appropriate relation [S-T14-007]. The record classifies this carefully. It is supported source attribution, replacing an earlier reconstruction that was the project's own. Its claimed necessity remains contested. And the modal step, which generalizes from the conditions of a recognizably human character to endless human existence, is flagged as the argument's contested bridge. Williams addresses endless human life. To apply his argument to a strict timeless foundation or to transformed persons needs additional premises.

Fischer's reply, inspected in a later reprint layout, contests each pillar of the challenge without claiming more than it can. He distinguishes the identity condition from the attractiveness condition. He separates experiential content that is absorbed in the outer world from ownership of the experience, and he rejects the automatic inference from absorption to loss of the subject. He argues that an appealing endless life could contain varied activities and periods of boredom. He distinguishes self-exhausting pleasures from repeatable ones, relative to a person and to how the pleasures are distributed. And he allows changes of character that arise through appropriate reflection to remain attractive, while excluding coercion and deception [S-T14-003]. The recorded limits are these. Possible desirability is not necessary goodness, objective meaning, physical feasibility or actual immortality. And the Williams passages embedded in Fischer are Fischer's reconstruction, not an inspection of the original.

The version note and the adjudication both matter here. The inspected Fischer copy is a reprint. Its pages (224–238), its editorial 1996 publication note and its 2004 translation all postdate the 1994 journal original. The Broadview 2009 anthology is a supported candidate edition; the PDF's 2022 dates do not date the argument; and the journal identity is supported by metadata, while an exact collation of proof against final text remains unresolved. All of this qualifies the attribution. None of it is a defect in the argument [S-T14-003]. The recorded adjudication of the exchange keeps both sides alive. Fischer's distinctions of variety, repeatability and ownership contest the claims of inevitable exhaustion and indiscriminate loss of identity. A strengthened response on Williams's side asks whether renewal preserves genuine engagement and prospective reasons, or merely suppresses awareness of impoverishment. Occasional pleasantness does not settle that question. Universal necessity is not established by the comparison. Conditional worthwhile endurance remains open.

The comparative conclusion that the record licenses is enhancement under premises, and never more than that. Keeping the same persons in worthwhile relationships and projects could preserve goods that extinction would lose, and could add significance. That assumes the same subjects, worthwhile content, costs that do not dominate, and an appropriate rule of comparison. It does not follow that every enduring life is better. The identity discipline of the previous chapter carries over unchanged. Preservation of information, of recognition or of a complete history need not be the original person's continuing subjectivity. So the value comparison cannot be settled by a model that preserves a record while ending its owner. Before possible worthwhile continuation can support any claim of survival, it requires relevant goods, an appropriate relation to the person, and actual further experience.

The consciousness-factory proposal and its predictions

The proposal that the research plan contributes turns on a distinction the record states in so many words. A universe that contains conscious life and a universe that was intended to produce conscious life are distinct hypotheses. The outcome alone supplies no observed designer preference and no intended domain. Observing that experience occurs, however richly it occurs, does not by itself distinguish among the declared ends of the discrimination design, which are production, development and preservation. Nor does it select an intended end over incidental occurrence. The factory metaphor is the research plan's name for the intended-production hypothesis. Nothing in the mere occurrence of consciousness selects it.

What an intentional reading requires is recorded as a debt in three parts. Purposeful creation can explain a cosmic role, conditional on agency, on an end, and on a causal or grounding link. The worth of that role needs a separate normative premise, because authorship supplies a purpose relation and not worth by definition. Arbitrary or harmful ends would still be purposes. The timeless-foundation constraint from the previous chapters recurs here. A strictly timeless foundation cannot acquire experiences in sequence in the same respect. So a timeless agent model owes a coherent account of intention, whether as whole-history intention or as temporal agency, and that account must be assessed by the same standards as rival primitive laws and value commitments.

Metz's 2000 article, inspected complete, supplies the groundwork of purpose theory together with its own boundaries. Purpose theory is defined as a conditional account of what could confer meaning. It is not a claim that God exists or that any purpose has been assigned, and it can permit meaning without an afterlife [S-P2T13-003]. The article distinguishes a purpose assigned from a purpose fulfilled. It separates the grounding account from epistemic questions about which purpose is meant and how to fulfill it. It argues that some versions avoid the disrespect objection by making a respectful request for free moral action rather than manipulating, while it rejects an infinite-damnation version outright. These are conditional analyses resting on contested normative premises. No actual purpose is verified anywhere in the chain.

Metz's own principal objection and his qualifications are kept together, because later commentary has blurred them. The 2000 article frames its objection around the claim that God is necessary for any meaning, which it explicitly distinguishes from the possibility that God enhances meaningful lives. It proposes that uniquely valuable divine qualitative properties best explain strict divine necessity, while prima facie conflicting with purposiveness [S-P2T13-003]. The same original already says that the objection does not require disbelief in purpose theory, and it presents two reply strategies. So the concessions of 2015, that he did not conclusively establish the incompatibility of atemporality with purposiveness or the exclusivity of his preferred divine properties, are continuous with the 2000 text. They are not a wholesale retraction [S-T14-004]. Poettcker's and Affolter's originals remain uninspected, and no direct reply coverage is invented in their place.

The testable form of the factory proposal is a discipline of design, not a result. Production, development and preservation are different proposed ends. Each must be paired with a declared population, means, constraints and tradeoffs, because an outcome weakens a model only when fixed commitments made in advance exclude or disfavor it. Observer selection belongs in every comparison, since conscious observers necessarily observe from a conscious perspective. And the same data must be run across intentional, impersonal-teleological and incidental models. The recorded objection is kept. Unknown goals and resources can accommodate almost any outcome, so unrestricted hidden purposes discriminate weakly. Only tightly constrained variants face qualitative tests. No population survey, likelihood assignment or completed test exists in the record, and the analysis of suffering is deferred to chapter 10.

The dependence warning applies across this chapter and its neighbors. Value premises about consciousness, and the same observed experiences reused in arguments about purpose, survival and foundation, are not independent evidence. A life that motivates a hope of survival, grounds a claim about meaning and figures in a design argument is one item of evidence, not three. And the three claims that the factory proposal is tempted to run together stay separate in the record. An occurrence of consciousness, an intended role and a worthy role are distinct things. Duration or value alone establishes none of the agency, intention and normative bridges.

Finite meaning and valuable endurance are compatible

The article's constructive conclusion, which this chapter has now grounded record by record, is that neither position licenses a blanket verdict. An attractive possible continuation need not make compulsory immortality desirable for everyone. The possibility of boredom need not make every extension worthless. Finite meaning and valuable endurance are compatible claims. Wolf's account and the finite-value argument ground a kind of significance, under an explicit value premise, for which duration is no part of the proposed explanation. The enhancement reconstruction and Fischer's replies keep worthwhile continuation open as a conditional possibility. A conscious foundation could enhance significance by sustaining worthwhile lives. It need not be invoked to rescue every finite good from meaninglessness.

Notice what the compatibility conclusion does. It removes a premise; it does not add one. It removes one reason for treating cosmology as a prerequisite of ordinary significance, namely the strict necessity thesis, which no inspected author successfully defends. Yoshizawa rejects the general claim that a person's death itself makes her life meaningless, and Metz proposes a stricter formulation without endorsing its soundness. Removing that premise does not trivialize the harms of death, which the final-loss argument locates in prevented goods and frustrated projects. And it adds nothing to the metaphysical ledger: no enduring bearer, no actual continuation, no cosmic intention, no worthy purpose. The value question and the existence questions remain just as separable as the article claims. Chapters 5, 7 and 10 carry the existence questions.

Dependence accounting, access gaps and non-conclusions

The dependence structure is authorial and small. Each inspected author is one group. There is the Craig essay, the Wolf lectures across their two inspected versions, and the Fischer reprint. There is the Williams chapter, independently inspected as a complete original. There are the Metz texts (the 2000 article, the 2015 reply, and the body-unavailable 2013 chapter), and Yoshizawa's independently inspected 2015 study. And the project's bridge inferences form a group of their own. Two notes about chains qualify the census. The Williams passages embedded in Fischer are a reconstruction limit inside the Fischer group; they are not an entry path for the Williams original, which stands on its own complete inspection. The Metz 2013 position is triangulated entirely through Yoshizawa and Metz 2015, which makes it one reporting chain about that chapter with no independent check. Nothing in this chapter is an empirical sample. No group contains an observation that could be double-counted into the empirical chapters, and the reused-premise warning of c08-p023 is itself the accounting rule.

The access gaps are declared, and the non-conclusions close the chapter. Metz 2013 chapter 7 remains identity-verified and body-unavailable. Poettcker, Affolter, Yoshizawa's Japanese 2011 paper, Metz 2003, Tartaglia, the second Wolf lecture with its commentaries, and the Stump/Kretzmann, Rogers and Lodzinski bodies referenced in Metz 2000 all remain uninspected. The Fischer reprint's exact edition and its journal collation remain unresolved. The Craig essay's publication date is not displayed. Let me be clear about what this chapter has not shown. It cannot conclude that significance requires endurance, because the necessity thesis lacks a defended bridge. Nor can it conclude that endurance is worthless or undesirable, since conditional worthwhile continuation survives the exchange. It cannot conclude that any life's meaning is actually enhanced by a conscious foundation, that the universe is or is not a consciousness factory, or that any purpose exists, let alone a worthy one. No component of the fundamental-consciousness package (fundamentality, universal subjecthood, brain independence, survival beyond irreversible death, timelessness, preservation, intention, goodness, moral authority) receives support from this chapter. All twelve withheld stronger-conclusion gates remain withheld through it.

9Moral Authority and Its Grounds

The previous chapter separated finite meaning from valuable endurance, and it left the value of a conscious foundation conditional. This chapter takes up a harder question, the normative one with which the article's seventh section closes. Does anything in a consciousness-first metaphysics, or in its rivals, supply moral authority? Moral authority here means the relation under which one agent's interests give another agent binding reasons. The chapter expands the second half of article section 7 through three packets. The first is the moral-grounding audit's Craig and Railton attributions, together with the project's own normative-bridge inferences. The second is the Phase 2 inspection of the Korsgaard and Nagel exchange on constructivism and the scope of reasons. The third is the Phase 2 inspection of the Enoch and Lenman exchange on robust realism and indispensability. Component boundary H-A binds throughout: consciousness, unity, creation, power and endurance do not automatically supply binding reasons. That boundary is the project's declared boundary, and what this chapter documents is that the inspected records leave it standing: none of the five items is shown to supply binding reasons automatically, and no inspected argument establishes consciousness as their unique ground. It is not a theorem about the world. The evidential challenges to a good foundation, suffering and hiddenness, belong to the next chapter. This chapter asks where obligation itself could come from.

The standing caution applies here, and this chapter adds its own version limits. The Korsgaard and Nagel texts were inspected as selected original chapters in a downloaded teaching copy of the 1996 Cambridge volume (fourteenth printing 2010). The marginal teaching annotations were excluded as non-author text, and no whole-book inspection is claimed. The Enoch book is a partial inspection. Chapter 1 came from a supplied preview; chapter 3's opening and concluding pages, chapter 4 complete and section 5.1 complete came from user-enabled page images. Pages 53 to 83 of chapter 3, which contain the developed indispensability case, are missing. The Lenman article is a complete published PDF. Enoch's 2014 reply was inspected in its Lenman-relevant sections, with the Manne and Sobel branches unread. The Craig material is an author-hosted 2022 podcast transcript, and the third parties it quotes were not independently inspected. Every substantive position below is an attribution, and the truth of its premises remains contested. Nothing in this chapter is an established moral or metaphysical truth, and no fundamental-consciousness component receives support.

From explaining agents to what they owe one another

The article's framing sets the burden. An account of why beings exist is not yet an account of what they owe one another. Even shared consciousness would not settle whether another subject's interest supplies an overriding reason for me. So the question is not whether a metaphysics can produce agents, or even valuable agents. The question is whether it can explain the normative relation between them. The article's comparison finds each of its three stopping points, constructivism, realism and conscious authority, owing something at this very point.

Consider first the strongest integrated case for grounding morality in consciousness, as the audit records it. It is a chain of bridges, and each bridge can be contested on its own. Start with subjects for whom conditions can be good or bad. The case must then assume that reasons to protect such subjects are objective, and it must propose one necessarily good personal foundation whose relation to agents grounds binding demands. Its bridge principles ask for four things. Valence must have normative significance. Relevant others must count even against my own aims. The foundation must be the relevant good whose relation supplies legitimate authority. And its commands must be identifiable and consistent with that nature. The audit's conclusion is qualified twice over. The full account could unify welfare, reasons, obligations and accountability if every bridge is warranted; and consciousness alone proves none of the later bridges. The objection the audit records is that calling a being good or creative may presuppose the very normativity being explained. The reply it records is that grounding need not be definition or temporal production; it may be identity or constitution. But an identity or constitution account still owes warrant for the identity and for the relation.

The divine-command position is attributed to its source, and its own self-imposed limits travel with it. Craig grounds value in God's nature and obligation in commands from a qualified authority. On his account the reasons for a command do not themselves constitute the obligation. He permits contingent commands consistent with essential goodness, and in doing so he corrects his earlier necessary-command formulation [S-T15-001]. The inspected text is a 2022 author-hosted podcast transcript. The authors Craig quotes or discusses there were not independently inspected, and the audio alignment is unverified. Both claims are recorded as attribution only, not as established moral or metaphysical truths. It should also be noted that the inspected transcript contains no independent proof of the authority relation itself.

The project's own bridge inferences say what the package still owes, and they serve as this chapter's recurring measure. There are four. First, creator status, causal dependence and superior power do not entail moral authority without a normative relation, and necessary goodness and reliable command identification require their own warrant. Second, consciousness can supply subjects of benefit and harm; but to identify valenced experience as reason-giving, and to extend concern across subjects, requires explicit normative bridges. Third, shared subjecthood could align prudential incentives without establishing impartial obligations, permissible tradeoffs or the protection of distinct persons. Fourth, personal endurance may enable eventual accountability and restoration, yet neither duration nor sanctions explains why an act is wrong. All four are project-authored conditional analyses, and they are marked as such.

Constructivism and the reflective bridge

Korsgaard's proposal, inspected in the original chapters, is the article's constructivist bridge. An agent must take some consideration as a reason, and practical identity helps determine which considerations can be endorsed. She grounds practical authority in reflective agency, without treating reflective self-conception as proof of a metaphysical self. She links reasons and obligations to practical identity, while distinguishing value from right action and from authoritative requirements. She argues that valuing anything requires valuing humanity as the source of our contingent identities. And she uses the shareability of reasons to defend obligations to others [S-P2T06-001]. Notice the shape of the argument. It seeks to connect commitment to one's own humanity with recognition of humanity in others. It does not derive obligation from sheer power or causal origin, and that is the very derivation the previous section found missing.

Korsgaard states her own limits, and they matter because the exchange turns on them. She distinguishes formal universalization from a moral law ranging over all rational beings; the first argument does not by itself establish the interpersonal domain. The humanity argument's extension to other persons requires an additional argument. She extends moral standing to conscious animals without making them reflective agents, and she does so on illustrations that are not independently verified empirical evidence in this packet. She denies that bare reflective endorsement makes every action right, since psychological explanation alone is not her criterion of justification. And she permits conflicts between obligations, while expressly not disproving complete practical normative skepticism [S-P2T06-001]. A theory this candid about its remaining steps does something useful. It supplies a map of what any successor, including a consciousness-based one, would have to complete.

Nagel's objection, also inspected, reverses the direction of explanation. He disputes that free self-determination must have a law-like form merely because it is causation, and he offers an external reflective standpoint as an alternative explanation of generality. He then argues that reasons ground practical identity rather than derive from it. A reflective egoist can understand a public demand while disputing its force; public intelligibility is not obviously impartial justification [S-P2T06-002]. His realism enters the record as a substantive evaluative rival. It is not a proof by rejection of Korsgaard, and its own warrant is just as contested.

Korsgaard's actual reply, inspected in the same volume, comes with its scope stated. She argues that general commitments constitute willing, which distinguishes genuine choice from a mere succession of impulses. She expressly limits this new argument to formal universality, and at the recorded page she adds no new defense of humanity-wide scope or of publicity. She distinguishes an action's incentive from its endorsement, denying that identity-based motivation means seeking a reward. And she locates the justificatory work in endorsement. She answers the attributed Mafioso objection with the claim that fuller reflection supplies a deeper moral demand. She retains a sense of nonmoral obligation under incomplete reflection, and Cohen's original chapter remains uninspected [S-P2T06-003]. The adjudication recorded in the packet is this. The reply answers a formal-agency objection, but it does not independently settle every bridge from agency to impartial moral authority.

What does the exchange contribute to this project? A narrowed gap, not a winner. The Phase 2 packet's closing inference makes two points. The selected reply leaves the direction-of-justification dispute open, and Nagel's alternative, on which evaluative reasons independently justify self-conceptions, is still live. And the developed rational-grounding proposal narrows the source gap of the first version, while establishing neither a fundamental conscious foundation nor its moral authority. No new evidence of a universal subject, survival, timelessness, preservation, cosmic intention or foundational goodness enters through this exchange.

Robust realism and indispensability

Enoch's robust realism, inspected in the book's first chapter, is the article's second stopping point. It holds that there is objective, irreducibly normative truth, and that attitudes may matter normatively without constitutively determining the fundamental normative truths. Two separations, both recorded, discipline everything that follows. Realist ontology is separated from epistemic warrant, so robust realism can coexist with normative skepticism. And Enoch himself denies that his objectivity and metanormative-realism arguments, even taken together, entail robust metaethical realism [S-P2T07-001]. The position enters this chapter as a characterization. It is not a proof.

The indispensability route has a declared inspection gap at its center. Enoch's introduction presents deliberative indispensability as on a par with explanatory indispensability, and as supporting belief in irreducible normative facts. The chapter that develops the argument was inspected only at its opening and at its concluding page. The opening targets general normative realism, not moral realism. The concluding page makes the claimed reason defeasible, and it acknowledges that full support requires rejecting alternatives not completely addressed there. Pages 53 to 83, including the epistemological derivation, remain uninspected [S-P2T07-001]. As the packet records it, the argument has three steps. Deliberation is rationally non-optional. Normative truths, and belief in them, are instrumentally indispensable to that project. And indispensability to an appropriate non-optional project can justify ontological commitment. Each step is contested in the exchange reported below.

Lenman's countercase, also inspected, asks whether the work can be done with something thinner. His schmeliberation takes reflectively scrutinized contingent concerns as its success standards, and it claims a deliberation-like phenomenology without robust truth. The challenge is that non-optionality and a thickness that requires robust truth cannot both be satisfied at once; only the thinner practice is indispensable [S-P2T07-002]. Two further notes are recorded. His expressivism differs from descriptive naturalism. And he distinguishes psychological truth conditions from consciousness constitutively determining normative truth conditions; that is a conditional normative independence, and it is not evidence about fundamental consciousness. It should also be said that no population finding about how people deliberate is established by the phenomenological claims on either side.

Enoch's reply, also inspected, defends a remaining claim to justification without turning it into truth. Similarity to choosing does not establish identity with deliberation. Success standards may lack deserved allegiance. Deliberative phenomenology and person-relative justification can vary, and he explicitly denies that indispensability makes normative truths true. His instrumental indispensability also includes a sufficiently diminishing reason to engage, which is revisable under contrary evidence [S-P2T07-003]. The reply defends the claimed reason. It is not a substitute for the uninspected developed case at pages 53 to 83. The packet also preserves a concession the article does not reach: Enoch acknowledges that he needs, but does not supply, a criterion that distinguishes legitimate Moorean reasoning from bootstrapping, and Joyce's original is uninspected. The upshot is symmetric. Lenman's alternative does not by itself remove Enoch's claimed reason to believe, and the deserved-allegiance demand may restate the disputed richness rather than resolve it.

The moral bridge in Enoch's fourth chapter, inspected complete, is expressly conditional and expressly weak. Assume moral objectivity, and assume a justified robust general normative realism. Then, he argues, the familiar motivations for withholding a specifically moral realism weaken: the metaphysical and epistemic objections generalize, and moral facts are proposed to add no new ontological kind. Yet Enoch explicitly denies entailment and allows that unconsidered motivations remain [S-P2T07-001]. The limits travel with the bridge. The cumulative-cost counterexample is acknowledged in the text. The detailed replies on metaphysics, knowledge, disagreement and motivation are deferred to chapters 6 through 9, which remain unread. And a local nonmoral expressivism is not ruled out. His treatment of categorical reasons distinguishes a rule's applicability despite indifference from an actual reason applying regardless of motivation, and in a recorded note he concedes that a successful Schroeder-style desire-grounded account could meet one sense of categorical. The moral rationalism he defends is the weak claim that morality's connection to reasons is not wholly contingent. There is no guarantee that every agent complies, there is no overriding priority, and exceptional cases are allowed. He does not require morality itself to be the source of the reasons. The record notes that this weak conclusion differs from Railton's separately acknowledged limitation, but does not by itself refute it. The antecedent chapters function here as assumptions; reading chapter 4 establishes neither of them.

The reduction question is left open by the book's own inspected section. In section 5.1 Enoch distinguishes conceptual from metaphysical irreducibility, and he distinguishes identity from reduction, grounding, constitution and type/token claims. He admits that the natural/normative framing is imprecise and that his argument is inconclusive against all naturalisms. He acknowledges that the familiar reduction objections are inconclusive and that his response to Schroeder does not prove irreducibility; the impasse, as he locates it, is over an initial intuition of normative force. And he remains open to abandoning that intuition for a sufficiently strong specific reduction, crediting naturalism's parsimony and flagging token-level reduction as worth developing [S-P2T07-001]. The originals of the critics he cites, Schroeder, the Moral Twin Earth literature, Smith, Fine and Parfit, remain uninspected; only Enoch's treatment is attributed. So robust realism enters the comparison with a foundational debt of its own, and its own defender has set the price.

Railton's naturalistic route, inspected in the original article, is the recorded secular alternative that takes the reduction option seriously. Other secular families are retained in the record. His non-moral good concerns objective interests: natural dispositional facts ground the idealized informed preference, and not the other way round. His substantive moral criterion is impartial consideration of affected interests under full information, and he himself labels that criterion controversial. He separates the applicability of obligation from categorical reasons for every rational agent, which his account does not guarantee [S-T15-002]. His reforming naturalistic definitions are recorded as theoretical proposals, and their empirical explanatory success remains a burden. The audit's conclusion holds. A physicalist ontology has a substantive route to moral realism. The route remains contested, and it does not settle every demand for categorical reasons: a fully informed egoist can still reject impartiality, and the recorded reply separates truth and applicability from the agent's motivation.

Conscious authority: the unmet relation

The article's diagnosis, which this chapter has now documented exchange by exchange, is simple. A theory that begins with consciousness does not dissolve the normative question, because explaining how a demand arises is different from explaining why it merits compliance. Korsgaard explains how reflective agents come to treat considerations as reasons. Nagel objects that this gets the direction of justification backwards. Enoch explains why deliberators may be entitled to believe in robust reasons. Lenman offers the same practice without the ontology. Railton builds normative properties from natural ones, at a price he names. A common-consciousness proposal faces the same fork. It can explain the genesis of demands through shared subjecthood, or it can assert their objective force. What the inspected records do not contain is any derivation of the second from the first.

The unmet relation can be stated plainly from the recorded bridges. What a conscious foundation would owe is not more consciousness but a normative relation. It would need warrant that the foundation is good in the reason-giving sense, and not merely powerful, creative or enduring. It would need warrant that its relation to agents legitimates demands, and does not merely enable sanctions. And what it requires would need to be identifiable. The recorded reply on behalf of the strongest case, that grounding can be identity or constitution rather than definition or temporal production, remains a live option; but it still owes warrant for the identity and for the relation. This is component boundary H-A applied. It is not a refutation. The records leave open that such warrant could be supplied; what they document is that nothing inspected supplies it.

What about the prudential shortcut? It is recorded as insufficient. If all subjects were one subject, harming another might become imprudent. But shared subjecthood that aligns incentives is not impartial obligation, and the audit's analysis is explicit that unity does not by itself establish permissible tradeoffs or the protection of distinct persons. Sanctions and endurance likewise enable accountability without explaining wrongness. The objection the audit records against the strongest integrated case makes the same point from inside it. Unity and sanctions can yield only self-interest. An account on which wrongdoing is ultimately self-harm has therefore moved from obligation to prudence; it has not derived one from the other. The normative gap survives the metaphysical merger.

The dependence rule for normative premises ties this chapter to its neighbors. The audit's synthesis argument records that the value judgments which support the moral-grounding proposal also underwrite the objections from evil. So the same moral premise must not be counted as independent evidence each time it recurs, and the standards for explanatory stopping points and for epistemic access must be applied symmetrically to divine and secular accounts. That mapping assigns burdens to the goodness and authority components, with conditional effects on intention, preservation and survival. It identifies neither a numerical posterior nor a winning full metaphysics. The evil and hiddenness exchanges that test the goodness component belong to the next chapter. What carries forward from here is the symmetry rule itself, which binds this chapter and chapter 10 wherever the same moral premises recur. The endurance claims this chapter shares with chapter 8 rest on their own recorded supporting claims; that is a separate relation.

Comparative stopping points

The article's comparative conclusion stands as recorded. The normative comparison remains substantive, and it does not have to wait for a verdict on fundamental ontology. Constructivism owes a bridge from agency to the scope and force of reasons; that is Korsgaard's own explicit remainder. Realism owes warrant for its normative starting point; that is Enoch's own impasse, priced by him. Conscious authority owes a relation between its nature and legitimate claims on agents; those are the audit's unmet bridges. None of the three gains an exemption by naming its stopping point. The article's further point also holds in the records: a successful metaphysics of consciousness could illuminate the bearers and objects of value, and still need to explain why these reasons apply and when they override others.

What the comparison does not license matters as much as what it does. No account is declared the winner. Nagel's realism is not vindicated by Korsgaard's concessions. Korsgaard's constructivism is not refuted by Nagel's objection. Enoch's realism survives Lenman's countercase only as an undefeated claim to justified belief. Railton's naturalism remains a contested proposal. The audit's earlier matrix reconstruction of constructivism and non-natural realism, recorded at the time with no decisive original defender inspected, is not withdrawn. The Phase 2 original inspections now supply this chapter's constructivist and robust-realist attributions and narrow that source gap, while the corresponding gap register entry remains partial and open. Each family's stopping point remains a commitment, and nothing in the evidence ledger settles the choice among them.

Dependence accounting, access gaps and non-conclusions

The dependence structure of this chapter is authorial, with one caution about a shared container. The Craig transcript is one group and the Railton article another. The Korsgaard and Nagel exchange is a further group: its three inspected texts (two Korsgaard contributions and Nagel's) sit in one edited 1996 volume and one teaching-copy container, and they are treated as one exchange-level group. The Enoch line (the book plus his 2014 reply) is a group, and so is the Lenman critique. The project's normative-bridge and comparative-bridge inferences are groups of their own. Enoch's book and his reply are one author's position, not two witnesses; Korsgaard's reply and her chapters are likewise one. No empirical sample appears anywhere in this chapter. The reused-premise rule of c09-p022 governs the normative premises this chapter shares with chapter 10, and the Phase 2 exchange's own adjudication inferences sit inside the exchange group rather than in a separate bridge group.

The access gaps are declared, and each carries its cost. The largest is in Enoch. Pages 53 to 83 of his chapter 3, which hold the developed indispensability argument and its epistemological defense, remain uninspected; this is the gap that matters most in this chapter, and the page 52 footnote continues into the missing pages. His chapter 2 (assumed by the chapter 4 bridge), chapters 6 through 9 (where the detailed replies are deferred) and section 5.2's fictionalism and error-theory branch are likewise unread, and first-printing byte equivalence for the book is not established. The Manne and Sobel branches of his reply, Joyce's original, Schroeder's original, and the Moral Twin Earth, Smith, Fine and Parfit texts are uninspected, as is Cohen's original chapter behind the Mafioso objection. The authors quoted in the Craig transcript (Baggett, Huemer, Shafer-Landau, Robert Adams) are uninspected, and its audio alignment is unverified. The Korsgaard and Nagel inspection covers selected chapters of a teaching copy, not the whole volume. The Railton PDF is a third-party archive copy with one noted footer anomaly, no established content difference, and no completed comparison against a journal-hosted copy. None of these gaps is silently bridged in the text above.

Let me be clear about what this chapter has not shown, and cannot conclude. It cannot conclude that moral realism is true or false, that constructivism succeeds or fails, that categorical reasons exist, or that any account of moral authority is correct. It cannot conclude that a conscious foundation has moral authority, and it cannot conclude that it lacks it. What it can say is only this: consciousness, unity, creation, power and endurance do not automatically supply binding reasons, and the inspected arguments establish neither consciousness as the unique ground of reasons nor a completed relation from a foundation to its authority. That is component boundary H-A, stated as a finding about the ledger and not about the world. Korsgaard's grounding of practical authority in reflective agency, reported earlier in this chapter, is the constructivist remainder that stands beside that boundary; it is not evidence that no derivation from consciousness was ever offered. No component of the fundamental-consciousness package receives support from this chapter: not fundamentality, universal subjecthood, brain independence, survival beyond irreversible death, timelessness, preservation, intention, goodness or moral authority. And all twelve withheld stronger-conclusion gates remain withheld through it.

10Goodness as an Explanatory Constraint

The previous chapter located the authority question. Nothing inspected derives binding reasons from consciousness, unity, creation, power or endurance. This chapter takes up the goodness question that the article's eighth section poses: what follows when a conscious foundation is specified as benevolent? It works through the moral-grounding audit's evil and hiddenness material and the Phase 2 deepening of both exchanges. It covers the evidential argument from suffering in its strongest inspected form. It covers the Adams and Sutherland exchange on horrendous evils and person-level defeat, including the revised Adams reproduction. It covers the Schellenberg and Howard-Snyder hiddenness exchange, including Schellenberg's actual 1996 response. And it covers the two internal costs the article names, survival reliance and inscrutability symmetry. Component boundaries H-G and H-I enter as the project's declared boundaries. The closing section prices them as findings about what the records leave unmet, not as theorems about the world. The symmetry rule for normative premises, carried forward from the previous chapter, governs throughout: the value judgments that support a moral-grounding proposal also underwrite the objections from evil, and neither side may count the same premise twice.

The standing caution applies here, and this chapter has its own version limits. The Rowe article was inspected in a university course copy, with OCR and visual checks on the decisive pages. The Adams and Sutherland symposium contributions sit in one 27-page PDF; that is a common-container dependency, with separately authored parts and some OCR-truncated pages checked visually. The revised Adams text is an author-attributed reproduction, and its exact 1990 print identity and equivalence remain unverified. The Schellenberg hiddenness argument was inspected in a 2025 author-hosted prepublication manuscript, which substitutes for an inaccessible 2004 chapter and carries manuscript rather than journal pagination. The Howard-Snyder article and Schellenberg's 1996 response are complete user-supplied journal articles. Every position below is an attribution, and the truth of its premises remains contested. Nothing here is an established theological, psychological or moral fact, and no fundamental-consciousness component receives support.

Benevolence as a testable specification

The article frames the matter as a trade, and the trade is worth stating plainly. Adding goodness to a conscious foundation increases both its attraction and its vulnerability. A life-valuing agent might explain why conscious beings arise. But suffering and nonresistant nonbelief then become relevant to what such an agent would permit. The objections that follow target specified benevolence, knowledge, power or openness to relationship. They need not undermine every possible conscious ground in order to weaken a particular providential explanation. This is the discrimination discipline of the meaning chapter's factory design, applied to a named attribute package. A specified benevolent intention generates expectations, and expectations can be tested.

The evidential argument from suffering is taken from its inspected source, and its author's own concessions come with it. Rowe's argument targets an omnipotent, omniscient, wholly good being, and it does so through preventable suffering that lacks the relevant goods or the evil-avoidance constraint. He concedes that the hypothetical fawn does not prove gratuitousness; he claims rational support rather than certainty, and he permits rational theistic countergrounds. His permission conditions are stated as necessary rather than sufficient, with footnotes qualifying irreplaceability and equal-value cases [S-T15-003]. Three cautions apply. The fawn is a thought experiment, not a case record. Rowe's statements about the abundance of suffering are his premises, not a new empirical survey. And all three claims are recorded as attribution only.

The audit's reconstruction keeps the argument evidential rather than deductive. Suppose an agent who knows the relevant harms and the feasible alternatives, who can prevent them, and who is perfectly good. Suppose also suffering that appears to lack any dependence on goods, or on the avoidance of comparable evil, that would justify permitting it. The reconstruction then uses two bridges. Our inability to find such a dependence is defeasible evidence of its absence in the relevant domain. And a perfectly good agent does not permit harms without adequate justification, with the equal-value and irreplaceability qualifications specified. The conclusion the audit records is conditional: suffering challenges that attribute package if the evidential and normative premises hold. It is not a deduction from the mere occurrence of any evil. The objection it records is the limited-sample worry. Our grasp of goods, causal relations and feasible worlds may be too small to assess what an omniscient being could justifiably permit. The reply it records is that finite limits do not automatically erase all evidence. Compare what each hypothesis actually leads us to expect, and ask what independently supports the claimed limitations; and theistic countergrounds can rationally matter. No measured prevalence or likelihood is supplied anywhere in the record, and the free-will and soul-making originals beyond the inspected discussions remain declared gaps.

The proposed justifying goods are recorded with their scope limits. Freedom and developmental opportunity can be proposed as goods. But their value alone does not justify every harm, every denied opportunity or animal suffering; scope and feasible alternatives matter. This is a project-authored conditional analysis, and it prices the goods on its own terms. A good that would justify some harms does not automatically justify these harms, for these victims, when these alternatives are in view. The restoration and hiddenness defenses below are measured differently, by their own records of distinct burdens and comparative means.

The symmetry rule takes a specifically epistemic form here. Unknown divine reasons can weaken an inference from unrecognized purpose. But unrestricted skepticism about our access to divine reasons weakens positive claims of recognizable divine goodness just as much. The cost depends on the scope of the skepticism. The discipline the audit records is local skepticism rather than blanket skepticism: name what remains knowable, and test whether the positive and negative inferences genuinely rely on the same inaccessible domain. This constraint returns at the chapter's close as the article's second internal cost.

Horrendous evils and person-level defeat

Adams's response to horrendous evils, inspected in the original symposium contribution, is recorded as the morally stronger form of reply, and the reason is where it locates the repair. She requires goodness within each participant's life, not merely a favorable global total. She distinguishes outweighing from an integration that defeats a horror's threat to personal meaning, and here she invokes divine intimacy. And she argues that internal consistency can be defended through how persons are restored, without known divine reasons why; she acknowledges that the account is conceptually underdeveloped [S-T15-004]. As the article puts it, this keeps the victim in view as the subject whose life requires repair, rather than an impersonal balance of aggregate goods.

The Phase 2 inspection sharpens what Adams is doing and what she is not doing. Her project is an internal consistency test. It uses the relevant religious attributes and valuables together with the kinds and amounts of evil, and availability within the system does not independently establish actuality. Her 1989 text already states an objective, individually relative criterion of horror that covers both doing and suffering; so the later clarification cannot be credited with first introducing objectivity. She distinguishes global goods from goodness to each created person, and she requires defeat within each participant's life, perpetrators included. She retains reasons for permission, while arguing that to specify how God could be good to each participant may exhibit compossibility without identifying why permission occurs [S-T15-004]. The limit the packet records is decisive for what follows. An unknown why is not no reason, and how-restoration does not by itself demonstrate morally adequate permission.

Her constructive proposals are recorded with their assumptions in plain view. She distinguishes engulfing a horror's threat from meaningful defeat through divine intimacy, and she proposes alternative Christological or mystical integrations. These alternatives carry different assumptions, and there is no observed restoration, no verified survival and no cumulative corroboration behind any of them. She acknowledges conceptual underdevelopment concerning shared pain and Christological metaphysics, and her cited further work is uninspected in this packet [S-T15-004].

Sutherland's criticism, inspected in the same volume, presses on what integration cannot settle. He distinguishes reassurance, or a worthwhile outcome, from the justice of the earlier harm. He presses the claims of other victims and the preservation of moral judgment: reassurance experienced by one observer cannot settle other victims' claims for them [S-T15-005]. His strongest objection is recorded as surviving his own disputable first-person reading of Adams. A printed-317 pagination anomaly is preserved in the record and not silently corrected. The two contributions share one PDF container, and the accounting section carries that dependence fact.

The revised Adams reproduction answers part of the criticism, within declared version limits. The inspected reproduction explicitly clarifies that horror gives everyone a prima facie reason for concern about the participant's life. It answers Sutherland's reason/emotion criticism by presenting intimate relationship as a good that supplies a reason to value the participant's life as a whole. And it denies that defeat entails that the original evil lacked horror, injustice or seriousness, or that moral intuitions about it were unreliable [S-T15-004]. Each claim carries the same limit. This is an author-attributed reproduction whose exact 1990 print identity remains unverified, and denying an alleged implication is not demonstrating actual defeat or adequate permission. The inspected passage that answers Sutherland also contains a visibly incomplete first proposition, which has been preserved rather than silently completed.

The recorded adjudication of the whole exchange separates three burdens. The article states Sutherland's other-victims point in its own sentence, and it then closes on a two-item distinction between a possible mode of defeat and actual restoration with justified permission. The inspected exchange narrows the reply gap. But it leaves participant restoration, observers' reassurance and the justification of the original permission as distinct burdens. Restoration can improve an account of what becomes of victims, while an account of why harms were permitted remains independently contestable. The audit preserves both the objection and the reply. The objection is that a later good may quiet grief without making the original injustice permissible, and that one person's reconciliation cannot speak for another victim. The reply, in its strongest version, addresses each victim and distinguishes redemption from approval of wrongdoing; an internal consistency defense is entitled to its own specified goods, without claiming that their actual existence is thereby proved. Adams's later clarifications, and any reply to Sutherland beyond the reproduction, remain uninspected.

Hiddenness and the comparative question

Schellenberg's hiddenness argument enters through a chain of versions, and the record declares the chain. The inspected text is his 2025 prepublication précis, which substitutes for an inaccessible 2004 chapter. In it, perfect love is linked to an available conscious relationship whenever a person is capable and nonresistant, and belief is required for that specified relationship. The resistance in question must be causally relevant to the nonbelief, so unrelated culpability does not defeat the premise. His own scope limits are recorded too: the conclusion does not establish metaphysical naturalism, and it does not exclude every nonpersonal religious ultimate [S-T15-006]. The audit's reconstruction adds the conditional form. If the premises hold, the specified perfectly loving personal God is excluded; other ultimate foundations remain open.

The contested bridge is named in the record. The argument needs perfect love to entail availability at each relevant time, and not only eventual welfare or eventual access. The audit marks this as the point a defense must attack, for a reason to delay developing a relationship does not automatically explain withholding the conditions of being open to one. No observed distribution of dispositions, and no actual divine reasons for permission, appear anywhere in the record. Availability, fittingness and the no-worse-alternative criterion all remain disputed.

Howard-Snyder's defense, inspected complete, is recorded with its structure and its stipulations. He distinguishes immediate desire from all-things-considered desire. He explicitly modifies the earlier hiddenness argument with an overriding-reason qualification and a separate no-reason premise; that is his reconstruction, and the record forbids transferring it into Schellenberg's 1993 or 2025 wording. He grants inculpable nonbelief for the sake of argument. He stipulates, rather than establishes, a prima facie criterion that permits a means no worse than the alternatives, and he does not claim that unaided cognition identifies an actual sufficient divine reason. The record preserves a printed premise-number anomaly at page 439 without silent correction. His account distinguishes belief in God's existence from willing reciprocity; belief is not loving reciprocation. His central good is relational. Delayed disclosure might allow a person freely to own or to improve a disposition to love, and he distinguishes ownership of an initially unchosen love from the greater fittingness of a love voluntarily cultivated. The extension from his first disposition type to the other two is assumed without argument [S-T15-009].

His concessions are recorded as carefully as his defense. By his own note, the sketched reason fails for the willing seeker who recognizes deficient love and strongly desires improvement. A sufficiently fitting seeker escapes his objection, though our ability to identify such a seeker is disputed. The account is limited to temporary nonbelief. It leaves cultural distribution unaddressed. It presupposes survival if the delay lasts a whole earthly life. And it does not rule out eventual coerced love as a second-best possibility [S-T15-009]. None of these concessions is proof about the actual population, but every one of them prices the defense.

The epistemic fallback is the defense's last line, and the record splits it into two parts. Howard-Snyder's final move challenges the inference from our failure to conceive of justifying reasons to their absence. That clause is attribution. The recorded addition is a project inference: the fallback's relation to the positive recognition of goodness requires a scope argument. Local uncertainty about divine reasons does not automatically license blanket uncertainty, and blanket uncertainty would cost the theist's positive claims too. This is the same symmetry stated at c10-p007, now located inside the inspected defense.

Schellenberg's actual 1996 response, inspected complete, restructures the dispute rather than merely rebutting it. He disputes the inserted overriding-reason clause and the independent no-reason premise, and he proposes positive defeasible support for divine intention instead. He accepts a commitment to the absence of overriding reasons, and he accepts vulnerability to actual counterconsiderations; what he denies is any requirement to establish that absence by an independent Rowe-style inference [S-P2T08-001]. The packet's structural point is this. Accepting an entailed proposition differs from independently establishing it, so the target of Howard-Snyder's fallback depends on which argument is being defended. That distinction alone establishes no full positive case and no metaphysical conclusion, and the positive case of the 1993 book is referenced but not read.

His three substantive responses are recorded with their status as premises marked. First, he contests capacity in cases of certain powerless rejection, and against likely-rejection cases he invokes the manner of disclosure and continued help, with a note denying any inference to the acceptance of actual-world counterparts. Second, he argues that freely confirming love and choosing its continuation can occur after belief, and that neither timing changes the origin of the initial disposition. Third, he proposes improving fittingness within belief, distinguishes experienced absence from nonbelief, and disputes the human-lover analogy through four divine-human disanalogies [S-P2T08-001]. Every one of these is held in the records as a philosophical comparative premise. They are theological counterpossibilities, not observed psychology and not quantified likelihoods.

The recorded adjudication leaves the exchange genuinely open, and it does so under a chronology that the ledger requires and the article's summary does not supply. The selected exchange leaves positive warrant for universal access, and for comparative developmental means, contested. Neither the imagined benefits of delay nor the reply establishes actual divine conduct. Howard-Snyder's available internal replies (ownership versus fittingness, improvement versus perfection, prima facie versus conclusive) are defenses already present in his 1996 article. They are not a later rebuttal to Schellenberg's response, and no such later rebuttal was inspected. Notice the dates. The 1996 reply addresses the earlier Schellenberg formulation, while the 2025 précis emphasizes resistance causally relevant to nonbelief. The exchange is real, it is complete on neither side, and it is split across three decades of versions. The article's summary survives all of this. The issue is comparative: does the better-relationship rationale warrant withholding an available relationship, rather than improving the person within it? It is not whether spiritual development is conceivable.

Internal costs: survival reliance and inscrutability symmetry

The first internal cost is survival reliance, and the record states it as a circularity constraint. A delay that lasts an earthly lifetime relies on survival if the relationship is ever to occur; that is Howard-Snyder's own recorded limitation. The audit's synthesis premise makes the general rule explicit: claims of actual survival, or of intended moral education, need evidence apart from their usefulness in a reply. In other words, the survival that the hiddenness defense needs cannot be inferred from the defense's need for it. The empirical and continuity chapters of this thesis hold the survival record, and nothing in this chapter's material adds to it. The same rule binds the restoration defense. Adams's integration proposals presuppose continued same-person life and coherent divine intimacy. Both are recorded as contested, and neither is supplied by the defense that requires them.

The second internal cost is inscrutability symmetry. If unknown reasons answer every discrepancy between suffering or nonbelief and a good agent's supposed preferences, then the recognition of favorable evidence needs equal scrutiny. In the form the audit records it, unrestricted skepticism weakens positive claims of recognizable divine goodness at the same rate that it weakens the objections. The record is explicit that this is not a refutation of every appeal to limited human understanding. The reply it records distinguishes local skepticism from blanket skepticism, and it asks whether the positive and negative inferences rely on the same inaccessible domain. What survives is the article's constraint. A proposal may not use specified intentions to explain life while treating their apparent failures as wholly inscrutable.

The chapter's constructive point is the article's closing sentence, and it rests on the audit's design discipline. A benevolent unification must allow its claims about goodness to restrict what counts as an expected world. This is the same precommitment requirement that the meaning chapter recorded for the consciousness-factory comparison, now applied to the benevolence attribute: declared ends, declared constraints, and outcomes that can weaken the model because the commitments were fixed in advance. A specified benevolent foundation that predicts nothing is not strengthened by the evil and hiddenness records. It discriminates weakly. Until its ends, population, means, constraints and tradeoffs are fixed in advance, it cannot face the qualitative tests that tightly constrained variants can face. And the tests differ by specification. Specified developmental purposes and specified preserving purposes face different conditional tests, concerning deprivation, opportunity and same-subject preservation. That is the recorded reason why it is the specification, not the family, that the evidence engages.

What the objections accomplish is bounded on both sides. They weaken particular providential specifications if their premises hold. They do not undermine every possible conscious ground; that is Schellenberg's own recorded scope limit, and it is the article's too. Nothing in the record supplies a verdict of equal support between the specified benevolent foundation and its rivals, and nothing licenses the reverse inference, that surviving these objections confirms the specification. The recorded sensitivity note also carries forward. Rejecting moral realism would change both the moral argument and some formulations of the evil objections, though internal inconsistency arguments could still run on an opponent's own commitments.

Dependence accounting, access gaps and non-conclusions

The dependence rule for normative premises binds this chapter most directly of all. The value judgments that support the moral-grounding proposal of the previous chapter also underwrite the evil objections here. They are one set of premises, not two bodies of evidence.
