  • Chronology: reported crisis in 1999, with sources conflicting on the date (January 14 or February 3) and no exact-day documentation inspected; publisher editions listed May 2012; a 2021 manuscript is a retelling. Rescue interval reported as eleven to fourteen minutes on a rescuer's watch in the memoir, fifteen minutes in the transcript, a thirty-minute estimate in a 2013 news report, and thirty minutes in a compiled accident database whose injury field says near-drowning.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: every inspected account postdates the episode by years; the memoir names four rescuers and reports interviewing each, but no separately authored witness statement, interview transcript or information-exposure history was inspected.
  • Witness independence: one narrator; witness thoughts reach the record only through her.
  • Perceptions: an apparent external vantage on the rescue, calm surrender, intermittent returns to breathe, and seeming simultaneity alongside narrated sequence; attributed experience, external accuracy not established. Foreknowledge: assessed under the intensive-care record. Recovery: survival with reported testing and no brain damage is a narrated outcome, not a chart diagnosis; later saturation figures concern hospital care.
  • Ordinary-access alternatives: not measured for this case; the rescue intervals measure neither circulation nor oxygen delivery nor neural activity.
  • Unknowns: original memoir and earliest narrative; independent witnesses and exposure history; medical records and timestamped measurements; prognosis and interventions; primary critic full text; audio alignment of the transcript.
  • Classification: documented findings, the publication dates and the son's death date from a dated newspaper; corroborated testimony, none independent of the narrator; uncorroborated testimony, the rescue vantage, the intervals and the recovery; interpretation, survival and brain independence; unknown, everything in the previous row.

C-T04-002: Mary Neal, reported intensive-care episode and recovery

A reported spiritual encounter during intensive-care recovery after the river episode, a distinct episode with the same narrator and an overlapping reporting chain; the two are never summed. Source: the purported talk transcript only. Phase 2 reinspection accepted sixteen claims.

  • Earliest-form claim: the transcript passages at the recorded cue ranges; no earlier dated account inspected.
  • Chronology: first weeks after the rescue, as reported; subsequent months of pain, operations, rehabilitation and retrospective investigation, dates unverified; the medication word in the transcript is garbled.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: the foreknowledge claim, that the narrator learned during the crisis that her oldest son would die, was disclosed in every inspected source after his death in a roller-ski collision on June 21, 2009; the complete dated news article discloses no earlier prediction, which is a bounded content check; the memoir's own draft labels conflict (a spring 2009 first draft revised and saved June 21, against a Q&A calling the hours-before-death manuscript the first complete draft), and the transcript does not explicitly state same-day completion.
  • Witness independence: one narrator; early confidants are reported by her.
  • Perceptions: the intensive-care encounter, attributed. Foreknowledge: an attributed prediction with documented reporting chronology; no independently dated prefulfillment record, no fixed deadline, cause or scoring set, and no all-prediction denominator; the memoir also attributes to the son a childhood belief that he would not reach eighteen and then narrates his life past eighteen. Recovery: reported, undocumented.
  • Ordinary-access alternatives: retrospective assertion is consistent with ordinary memory and retelling; no case-specific mechanism is demonstrated either.
  • Unknowns: independent intensive-care dates and charts; any dated prediction record; audio.
  • Classification: documented findings, the death date; corroborated testimony, none; uncorroborated testimony, the encounter, the prediction and its timing; interpretation, foreknowledge; unknown, the denominators.

C-T06-001: Eben Alexander, 2008 meningitis and reported experience

A single 2008 illness and hospitalization with reported onset November 10, 2008; recalled spiritual phases and the later photograph recognition all belong to one reporting chain, in the Alexander dependence group with the recovery episode below. Sources: a purported talk transcript with unverified audio; a converted 2012 ebook with 2023 conversion metadata disclosing changed names and editorial assistance; a 2018 external clinical review; a 2013 magazine article; a defender's long reply; the author's current webpage; a 2011 research report page updated in 2014; a published skeptical criticism; and a treating physician's separately authored signed statement. Phase 2 reinspection accepted thirty-three claims.

  • Earliest-form claim: the earliest dated text inspected is the 2012 memoir; the 2012 AANS account was identified but inaccessible; the reported six-week, more-than-20,000-word account written before reading the literature rests on later statements, with no dated manuscript inspected.
  • Chronology: acute Escherichia coli meningitis with prompt antibiotics, coma with ventilation, and awakening reported on day 7 by memoir, physician statement and current page, against the 2018 review's extubation on day 6 and improvement on day 7; admission Glasgow Coma Scale 11 in the review against 8 in the memoir and a defender's account; laboratory timing differs across tellings; no electroencephalogram was recorded.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: the 2018 review reports disclosure on day 9 and retrospective corroboration of praying visitors reportedly present on day 5, inferring an experience interval within days 1 to 5, with no original interview dates and no validated exclusion of ordinary acquisition; the memoir identifies its temporal anchors as one absent-person report and six recalled faces and calls other simultaneity conjectural.
  • Witness independence: the memoir states that the author reconstructed the week's ordinary events from others because he remembers none of them; the physician's statement is separately authored but is not a chart; the 2018 reviewers report a review of records this project has not inspected; the photograph corroboration is a defender's 2013 retrospective, unblinded, family-circle interview.
  • Perceptions: the reported phases and the six faces, attributed. Foreknowledge or recognition: the birth sister's photograph, received four months after discharge and recognized the following morning during a reading of an analogous case, with the author already aware before the coma that a birth sister had died in 2000 and having reunited with birth relatives in 2007; no blinded comparison, no dated pre-photograph description. Recovery: remarkable recovery attested by the physician; mortality figures given without denominators or a calculation model; recovery is not a chart diagnosis of prior cortical destruction.
  • Ordinary-access alternatives: the attending physician, as reported in 2013, described chemical sedation and delirious responsiveness; the 2018 reviewers leave the relative contributions of infection and sedation undetermined while arguing against a solely drug-induced account; the memoir's own appendix says full-thickness cortical destruction is unnecessary to its argument.
  • Unknowns: original charts and interview recordings; experience-to-measurement synchronization; any independent dated pre-photograph description; the 2012 account; audio alignment.
  • Classification: documented findings, the diagnosis, treatment and awakening as attested and reviewed, and the absence of an EEG; corroborated testimony, the praying visitors on day 5 as retrospectively corroborated by the review; uncorroborated testimony, the experience contents, the timing of the recognition and the prior written account; interpretation, cortical incapacity during the experience, survival, a creator; unknown, the interval, the exclusion and the records.

C-T06-002: Eben Alexander, post-awakening confusion

A subsequent reported confused and delusional recovery phase, which the speaker describes as distinct in remembered quality from the spiritual experience; a comparator within the same reporting chain, not a second experience. Sources: the transcript and the 2018 review. Phase 2 reinspection accepted two claims.

  • Earliest-form claim: the transcript's description of confusion as remembered differently.
  • Chronology: November 2008, exact interval unresolved.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: retrospective; no contemporaneous note inspected.
  • Witness independence: the narrator.
  • Perceptions, foreknowledge, recovery: the phase is offered by the narrator as a contrast in remembered quality; the contrast is attributed and unmeasured.
  • Ordinary-access alternatives: sedation and delirium are documented in the reviews of the illness; their relation to this remembered phase is not measured.
  • Unknowns: as for the illness record.
  • Classification: uncorroborated testimony, the remembered contrast; interpretation, that the contrast discriminates the spiritual experience from delirium; unknown, the interval.

C-T19-001: the dentures case, patient B and nurse TG

One reported resuscitation at the old Canisius Hospital near Nijmegen, attributed to late 1979 with the date unverified, whose entire substantive content reaches the record through nurse TG; the anecdote's appearance in the Dutch cohort paper is a documentary link and not cohort membership. Sources: Smit's 2008 article, a 2008 companion interview summary, and the defenders' 2010 rejoinder, with the 1991 nursing-journal article and the 1994 interview transcript identified but uninspected. Phase 2 reinspection accepted twenty-seven claims.

  • Earliest-form claim: the 1991 article is the earliest known publication and was not inspected; the earliest inspected form is the 1994 interview as excerpted in translation and the 2008 article.
  • Chronology: the event probably in 1979 by inference; TG's retelling more than a week after the resuscitation; the 1991 article; the interview of February 2, 1994 with its transcript dated February 3; the 2008 article and the follow-up interview, published as a nine-page summary of a record exceeding thirty pages; the 2008 critic-and-reply set; the 2010 rejoinder, which states that the official medical records were destroyed.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: every patient detail is retrospective through TG; the intermediary who carried the story to the 1991 author did not participate in the resuscitation; no investigator ever interviewed the patient.
  • Witness independence: one principal witness across repeated interviews; the patient's account exists only as reported by the nurse.
  • Perceptions: the patient reportedly later identified the nurse who removed his dentures and described an unusual cart with a flat wooden pull-out shelf already extended, a description that specifically challenges a metal-drawer hearing explanation, while the Dutch interview article itself contains a colloquial drawer word; the published interview's pump-first passage was corrected by TG in her 2008 reply to denture removal before the compression device was switched on, with an estimate of more than five minutes of transport without cardiac massage that does not exclude earlier bystander or ambulance resuscitation. Foreknowledge: none claimed. Recovery: the patient survived; no clinical detail inspected.
  • Ordinary-access alternatives: hypothermia, resuscitation-restored awareness, sensation, hearing and later reconstruction proposed by the critic, who corrected his own earlier assumptions; the defenders' proposed ten-participant blindfold experiment was never performed; no mechanism demonstrated for the event.
  • Unknowns: patient interview; raw charts, tracings or independent dated perception or witness statements; the patient's full information access; the 1991 and 1994 documents and the Dutch 2008 originals; the remaining pages of the 2010 Dutch response.
  • Classification: documented findings, the publication chain and its dates; corroborated testimony, none beyond the single witness; uncorroborated testimony, the identification, the cart description and the corrected sequence; interpretation, extracerebral perception or ordinary hearing; unknown, timing, records and access.

C-T19-002: Pam Reynolds, 1991 surgical report

One reported August 1991 aneurysm operation under hypothermic circulatory arrest, described through Sabom's 1998 account and the later debate; one patient, one event, one reporting chain through one investigator. Sources: Woerlee's 2011 investigation, Hameroff's response, Carter's response, with the Sabom chapter and the 1994 interview identified by catalog metadata only, and the Phase 2 additions of Woerlee's rejoinder, Carter's further reply and Smit's letter. Phase 2 reinspection accepted thirteen claims.

  • Earliest-form claim: the 1994 interview and the 1998 chapter are the earliest forms, and neither was inspected; the case is known through critics and defenders who quote them.
  • Chronology: surgery in August 1991 with the day unverified; an interview several days later according to a defender; the 1994 interview; the 1998 book; the 2002 and 2011 debate; the rejoinder and the letter thereafter.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: a defender places the externally checkable perceptions, through Sabom, before the recorded absence of brainstem responses, with the relatives-and-light episode not independently timed; no prefeedback list of correct and incorrect perceptions exists; the monitoring technologist is quoted locating himself in the adjacent operating room and requesting an exact-parameter experiment.
  • Witness independence: the patient's account exists as quoted by Sabom; every later text reuses his account and the technical communications about one surgery.
  • Perceptions: unusually specific reported surgical tool and dialogue details; the checkable ones lie outside the alleged standstill. Foreknowledge: none claimed. Recovery: not at issue.
  • Ordinary-access alternatives: an ordinary-hearing account with drug effects, developed from related surgical protocols because the original account is nontechnical; the critic's rejoinder explicitly leaves the immaterial explanation unexcluded for lack of case data; a self-performed consumer sound demonstration found ambient sounds audible under intolerable clicks, which is neither the proposed institutional test nor a calibrated case-equivalent experiment; a webpage titled as a successful test remains uninspected after a stopped access route.
  • Unknowns: the full 1998 chapter and the 1994 interview; operative and anesthesia charts, drug concentrations and evoked-response time series; a calibrated case-specific hearing result; the complete prefeedback perception list.
  • Classification: documented findings, the published debate and the sound demonstration as reported; corroborated testimony, the technologist's location as quoted; uncorroborated testimony, the perceptions and their timing relative to the operative phases; interpretation, excluded-process perception or ordinary hearing; unknown, the charts and the interval.

Prospective studies

C-T07-001: AWARE I

A prospective multi-center study at fifteen hospitals in the United States, the United Kingdom and Austria, main enrollment July 2008 to December 2012, with a 2007 pilot excluded from the main sample. Source: the 2014 article, whose final flow figure was inspected in Phase 2. Phase 2 reinspection accepted five claims.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 2,060 recorded arrest events; 330 discharge survivors, with the event-to-person conversion unexplained; 140 stage-one interview completers, against printed counts of 52 in-hospital plus 90 post-discharge interviews and a table denominator of 142, an unresolved discrepancy that AWARE II's introduction compounds by citing 143; 101 stage-two completers, 39 non-completers; 55 with a positive memory response at stage one and 85 without; at stage two, 46 with no memory, 46 with non-NDE memories, 9 at the Greyson threshold including 2 with auditory or visual event recall; 1 of the 2 followed up, the other in failing health; interview timing 3 days to 4 weeks in hospital and 3 months to 1 year by telephone.
  • Findings: both event-recall cases occurred at locations without installed target shelves; the authors report medical-record corroboration for one case and infer approximately three minutes of awareness from a defibrillator sequence, with awareness up to three minutes and arrest lasting at least three minutes being different quantities; no participant-linked neural recording accompanies any reported content; the main study abandoned programmed auditory cues after the pilot.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: recollection at interview, days to a year after the event; corroboration from records for one case as reported by the authors.
  • Unknowns: separate eligibility and approach counts; the exposed-and-interviewed intersection; the 140 versus 142 discrepancy; no pooled rate.
  • Classification: documented findings, the flow counts and the shelf locations; corroborated testimony, the one record-corroborated recall as the authors report it; uncorroborated testimony, the remaining memories; interpretation, the three-minute inference; unknown, the intersections.

C-T07-002: AWARE II, prospective arm

A feasibility phase from April 2013 to May 2015, a five-site pilot to May 2017, and a twenty-five-site phase to March 2020, with consecutive recruitment during weekday daytime hours. Sources: the 2023 article and its excerpt, the Greyson and van Lommel letter with Parnia's reply, and the Martial letter with the authors' direct reply. Phase 2 reinspection accepted eighteen claims.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 567 recruited; 354 without sustained return of circulation and 213 with it; 160 hospital deaths after return; 53 discharge survivors, 44 with favorable outcome, 8 with severe disability, 1 vegetative; 28 interviewed and 25 not (4 refused, 7 disabled, 3 other, 11 unreported); 11 with any memory or perception, in overlapping categories, and 17 with none; 6 transcendent experiences at the Greyson threshold, with median scale score 7 and maximum 18; 2 with recollections of emergence during resuscitation and 2 after, 3 dreamlike; equipment deployed in 365 of 567, which is not verified stimulus exposure; EEG captured from 85 subjects and interpretable in 53, of whom 49 also had cerebral oximetry; 851 EEG images captured and 466 interpretable; exactly 2 interviewees with EEG data, neither among those with explicit recall.
  • Findings: no explicit recall of any image or auditory target among the 28 and no implicit visual identification; one correct three-fruit choice, for which the any-three-fruits request supplies no uniform chance model; one separate participant's overt recollection of clinical surroundings received chart verification of a shock event, with unmeasured prior resuscitation knowledge acknowledged; near-normal or physiological EEG rhythms reported up to 35 to 60 minutes into resuscitation on a limited frontotemporal montage during pulse-check pauses, with alpha-coma interpretation not excluded and suppression in 47 percent of EEG data, a data-unit figure and not a proportion of people.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: recollection at interview after discharge; aggregate rhythm timing cannot date any particular memory's formation; the published exchanges supply argument, not the missing intersection.
  • Unknowns: the supplement, its figures and its narratives; confirmed target exposure among interviewees; any participant, content and time linkage to a recorded absence of processing; exact category intersections and repeat-event handling; between-arm overlap.
  • Classification: documented findings, the flow counts, the deployment count and the EEG data-unit findings; corroborated testimony, the chart-verified shock recollection as reported; uncorroborated testimony, the remaining recollections; interpretation, the disinhibition account in the authors' reply; unknown, every opportunity intersection.

C-T07-003: AWARE II, community arm

A cross-sectional selected sample of community survivors who submitted narratives by mail or were identified from a public database on the basis of resuscitation history, with adult self-reported cardiac-arrest cognitive experience as an inclusion criterion; grounded-theory coding with a recoded subset. Sources: the 2023 article and its excerpt. Phase 2 reinspection accepted three claims.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 126 selected narratives; no all-arrest sampling denominator.
  • Findings: thematic categories only; the arm supplies no target trials and acquires no prospective EEG observations, and the typed links keep the two arms apart.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: retrospective narratives of unknown delay; no corroboration.
  • Unknowns: the supplement; overlap with the prospective arm and with previously published narratives, unknown and not asserted absent.
  • Classification: documented findings, the count and coding method; uncorroborated testimony, the narratives; unknown, the overlap.

C-T07-004: the Dutch prospective cohort

Ten Dutch hospitals from 1988 to 1992, four months to nearly four years per site, consecutive successfully resuscitated coronary-care patients. Sources: the 2001 article in its final published layout, and the 2002 correction, which changes one drug name in one sentence. Phase 2 reinspection accepted four claims.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 344 people and 509 resuscitation episodes, different units; 62 with a broad-definition experience (18 percent) and 41 core (12 percent), 282 without; 248 interviewed within five days printed as 74 percent, a figure whose denominator is unclear and is not manufactured; at two years, 37 initial experiencers (62 minus 19 deaths and 6 refusals) and 37 matched controls selected from 75 contacted, reclassified to 35 and 39; at eight years, 23 and 15; the same retained people throughout, not new cohorts.
  • Findings: the correction alters no cohort count or follow-up result; site selection is acknowledged as possible; arrest timing is estimated; no participant-specific EEG accompanies any reported experience; the dentures anecdote printed in the article is contextually linked and never added to the denominator.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: interview within days for most, by the authors' printed figure; longitudinal reclassification thereafter.
  • Unknowns: initial arrest count; a separate pre-inclusion count; the 74 percent denominator.
  • Classification: documented findings, the cohort counts and the correction; uncorroborated testimony, the experience reports; interpretation, the broad and core classifications; unknown, the initial-arrest denominator.

Historical documents

C-T08-001: Dryhthelm's narrated return (Bede V.12)

One narrated return from apparent death, in dependence group DG-T08-Bede-V with the two deathbed visions tabled next: Dryhthelm's illness, apparent death overnight and revival at dawn (V.12, editor heading around 696), transmitted to Bede through a named intermediary. Source: Bede's Ecclesiastical History, composed around 731, inspected in a 1907 revised English translation digitized in 2011; the Latin was not collated. No Phase 2 reinspection. The three Bede narratives share one author, one genre and one translation, and no one of them is a clinical control or an independent manuscript witness for another.

  • Earliest-form claim: Bede's text as translated; the Latin was not collated.
  • Chronology: narrated illness, apparent death overnight and revival at dawn; repeated interviews by Haemgils transmitted to Bede, dates unknown; composition around 731; translation 1907; digitization 2011.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: none contemporaneous; no clinical record, no independently inspected witness statement.
  • Witness independence: none; literary and religious selection throughout; the chain runs through the intermediary Bede names.
  • Perceptions: a luminous guide, punishment scenes, a pleasant landscape, return and lasting behavioral change, as narrated. Foreknowledge: none claimed. Recovery: narrated revival, with no clinical death established. Denominators: one inspected account, no population at risk, zero clinical cases verified by the project.
  • Ordinary-access alternatives: not assessable; the narrative is not a clinical event.
  • Unknowns: original manuscripts; any witness beyond the chain Bede names; the event date.
  • Classification: documented findings, the existence, date and content of the narrative; uncorroborated testimony, everything narrated; interpretation, any clinical reading; unknown, the event.

C-T08-002: the Mercian thegn's narrated deathbed vision (Bede V.13)

One fatal deathbed vision narrative, in dependence group DG-T08-Bede-V: a Mercian thegn's vision (V.13, editor heading 704 to 709), which Bede attributes to Bishop Pechthelm. It is a literary comparison account in the same work as the Dryhthelm narrative, not a surviving-experiencer interview. Source: the same 731 composition in the same 1907 translation. No Phase 2 reinspection.

  • Earliest-form claim: Bede's text as translated; the Latin was not collated.
  • Chronology: editor heading 704 to 709, with the date not independently established; the account attributed by Bede to Bishop Pechthelm; composition around 731; translation 1907.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: none contemporaneous; no clinical record, no independently inspected witness statement.
  • Witness independence: none; one attributed intermediary; literary and religious selection throughout.
  • Perceptions: a deathbed vision with an explicit admonitory purpose, as narrated; the record does not itemize its content. Foreknowledge: none claimed in the record. Recovery: none; the narrative is fatal. Denominators: one inspected account, no population at risk, zero clinical cases verified by the project.
  • Ordinary-access alternatives: not assessable; the narrative is not a clinical event.
  • Unknowns: original manuscripts; any witness beyond the attribution Bede gives; the event date.
  • Classification: documented findings, the existence, editorial dating and admonitory content of the narrative; uncorroborated testimony, everything narrated; interpretation, any clinical reading; unknown, the event.

C-T08-003: the unnamed monk's narrated deathbed vision (Bede V.14)

One fatal deathbed vision narrative, in dependence group DG-T08-Bede-V: an unnamed monk's vision (V.14), described by Bede as recent, concerning a monk Bede says he knew. Knowing the monk is not the same as independently observing the narrated events, and the record keeps the two apart. Source: the same 731 composition in the same 1907 translation. No Phase 2 reinspection.

  • Earliest-form claim: Bede's text as translated; the Latin was not collated.
  • Chronology: described as recent, exact date unknown; narrated fatal illness and report to the brethren; composition around 731; translation 1907.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: none contemporaneous; no clinical record, no independently inspected witness statement.
  • Witness independence: none; Bede's acquaintance with the monk is not an independent witness chain; literary and religious selection throughout.
  • Perceptions: a deathbed vision with an explicit admonitory purpose, as narrated; the record does not itemize its content. Foreknowledge: none claimed in the record. Recovery: none; the narrative is fatal. Denominators: one inspected account, no population at risk, zero clinical cases verified by the project.
  • Ordinary-access alternatives: not assessable; the narrative is not a clinical event.
  • Unknowns: original manuscripts; any witness beyond Bede's own statement; the event date.
  • Classification: documented findings, the existence and admonitory content of the narrative and Bede's stated acquaintance; uncorroborated testimony, everything narrated; interpretation, any clinical reading; unknown, the event.

Cross-cultural and retrospective collections

C-T08-004: the North Indian incidental collection, 1986

Sixteen included case reports from eighteen incidentally identified during reincarnation investigations, four narrated in detail as illustrations and not as additional cases, with a median investigation delay of about fourteen years. Source: the 1986 report. No Phase 2 reinspection.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 18 identified, 2 excluded as scant, 16 included; 10 direct subject interviews, 5 firsthand-informant cases without the subject, 1 secondhand; a subset of 4 with both subject and another informant; 11 male, 5 female; age known for 14; American comparison rows of 78 and, for two rows only, 106; no population or invitation frame.
  • Findings: messengers in 12 of 16 and mistaken-identity return in 10 of 16 as selected-case descriptions; the authors argue that cultural variation proves neither belief-only production nor an afterlife.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: long delays; no synchronization with neurological measurement.
  • Witness independence: subjects and informants as recorded; elicitation unequal between the Indian and American material, with comparison-table blanks meaning no instance learned and possibly never asked.
  • Unknowns: Hindi transcripts and a bilingual translation audit; the American source cohorts, not reopened; any prevalence.
  • Classification: documented findings, the counts and the elicitation method; uncorroborated testimony, the reports; interpretation, the cultural explanations; unknown, prevalence.

C-T08-005: the Japanese published-interview reanalysis, 2014

Twenty-two selected interviews from a 2003 Japanese book, with one excluded sailor hallucination interview and a preliminary literature pool of 273 cases not added; overlap among the underlying books unquantified. Source: the 2014 reanalysis. No Phase 2 reinspection.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 22 analyzed interviews, 1 excluded comparator; 14 male, 8 female; age known for 21; 273 literature cases not added; no recruitment frame or delay distribution.
  • Findings: heavenly places in 16 and borders in 12, with flower gardens in 13 of the 16 and rivers in 10 of the 12, against zero panoramic life reviews in the sample, with outside-sample counterexamples acknowledged.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: published interviews of unknown delay; none.
  • Witness independence: as published; the underlying interview books were not inspected, and no independent bilingual audit exists.
  • Unknowns: the original interviews and the Western originals; prompt coverage, coder agreement and translation validation.
  • Classification: documented findings, the motif counts in the sample; uncorroborated testimony, the interviews; interpretation, the cultural explanation; unknown, the sampling frame.

C-T08-006: the distressing-experience correspondence collection, 1992

Fifty self-selected accounts arising from about thirty spontaneous correspondents over ten years plus a newsletter request, with no full person-to-episode crosswalk and one example describing two childbirth episodes for one woman; in the IANDS retrospective dependence group with the two collections below. Source: the 1992 paper. No Phase 2 reinspection.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 50 accounts; about 30 earlier correspondents; total invited, unique people and unique episodes unknown; 3 typology classes with no full class counts.
  • Findings: three proposed types including void and hellish experiences, persistent despair alongside fear-to-peace transitions, and one account of endless succession; a candid discussion of positive-affect scale bias and reporting reluctance.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: varying delays, not tabulated; none.
  • Witness independence: self-selected correspondents; prior-book overlap and participant independence unestablished; no clinical documentation or sampling frame; no control arm.
  • Unknowns: the person-episode crosswalk, the invitation denominator, the class frequencies.
  • Classification: documented findings, the count and the typology proposal; uncorroborated testimony, the accounts; interpretation, the types; unknown, the denominators.

C-T08-007: the non-life-threatening and coma comparison, 2014

Voluntary recruitment through IANDS France and Flanders and Liège web and media channels; 190 scale-selected reports divided into 50 from non-life-threatening events and 140 from self-reported coma, with historical prospective comparisons that reuse earlier data. Source: the 2014 paper. Phase 2 reinspection accepted eight claims.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 215 submitted, 25 excluded for scores below 7, 190 included; 50 and 140 by group, subgroups not extracted; 42 historical prospective reports pooled as documented analytic reuse (27 from a 2003 study, 11 from a 2002 study, 4 from Parnia 2001), and a broader figure pooling 429 retrospective and 47 prospective reports from published datasets with unknown participant overlap; mean reporting delay 24 plus or minus 15 years; no invitation or all-survivor denominator.
  • Findings: no significant intensity or item-frequency differences between groups, which is not an equivalence result; cutoff wording internally inconsistent; clinical statuses medically uncontrolled self-report; a legend-year mismatch labeling the current study 2013 establishes no separate sample; its relabeling of Parnia's two bodily-awareness responses as out-of-body experiences is a scale-category use the original prose contradicts, qualified without altering any published number.
  • Witness independence: volunteers; overlap with the 1990 cohort is explicit analytic reuse in one figure, with no claim that the 190 new reports share participants.
  • Unknowns: original clinical records, uniform translations and individual data; a prospective matched non-crisis cohort.
  • Classification: documented findings, the counts and the reuse; uncorroborated testimony, the reports and their clinical statuses; interpretation, the equivalence reading, which is withheld; unknown, overlap.

C-T08-008: the self-described experiencer and denier comparison, 1990

Two hundred forty-six newsletter respondents to a questionnaire invitation about close brushes with death, 183 describing themselves as experiencers and 63 denying an experience, groups that are not identical to scale-defined categories; the time item was broadened. Source: the 1990 paper. No Phase 2 reinspection.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 246 respondents; 183 self-described experiencers, of whom 169 scored at least 7 and 14 below; 63 self-described non-experiencers, of whom 18 scored at least 7 and 45 below; mean delay 18.1 years for the experiencer label and 22.3 for the other; all invited and any random clinical population unknown.
  • Findings: the scale and the self-label pull apart in both directions; the broadened time item groups apparent simultaneity, stopped time and time losing meaning into one response category, so a high score is not a count of one uniquely defined timeless experience.
  • Witness independence: newsletter recruitment; possible overlap with other collections; the cohort is reused in the 2014 comparison's figure.
  • Unknowns: any random all-survivor sampling; validated absence of experience among deniers.
  • Classification: documented findings, the counts and the item wording; uncorroborated testimony, the self-labels and responses; interpretation, any temporal ontology read from the item; unknown, overlap.

Controlled and comparative studies

C-T09-001: the Cogitate adversarial collaboration

A suprathreshold visual experiment and its associated protocol, main results, supplement, critique and correction, all concerning one experimental program, in dependence group DG-T09-001. Sources: the 2023 protocol, the 2025 main article and supplement, the 2025 critique and its 2026 author-name correction, and the selected amendment file. No Phase 2 case reinspection; the protocol audit is recorded under the Phase 2 neural packet.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 256 recruited across fMRI (120), MEG (102) and intracranial EEG (34); 108, 97 and 32 retained; main noninvasive tests on 73 held-out fMRI and 65 held-out MEG datasets, with invasive data not split; a separate memory control of 39; the recruited, retained, held-out and optimization fields are carried at the values the chapter reports, and the amendment's 110 quality-passed datasets alongside a 36-of-80 division do not reconcile with the paper's 35 optimization and 73 held-out.
  • Findings: sustained posterior representations partly supported; transient prefrontal category decoding present; the predicted prefrontal offset profile and sustained posterior gamma synchrony not established; the preregistered pairwise-phase-consistency analysis supported neither critical connectivity prediction; an exploratory amplitude-based connectivity analysis partly supported the workspace expectation and is not preregistered confirmation; no theory winner.
  • Chronology: protocol published February 10, 2023, with the selected amendment file named v4 but internally Version 4.5 and dated December 22, 2022, containing one-third split wording against the protocol's half-split wording, a contrast that cannot date the change; main article and supplement April 30, 2025; critique October 9, 2025; correction May 1, 2026; no later consortium reply located by a bounded search.
  • Unknowns: the full OSF version history and analysis code; analyst data-access history.
  • Classification: documented findings, the counts, the outcomes and the declared analysis departures; interpretation, the critique's and the proponents' readings; unknown, the version history.

C-T09-002: the macaque central-thalamic stimulation study

Two rhesus macaques receiving deep brain stimulation under maintained propofol anesthesia, with three separate awake comparators and reused awake auditory data, out of five animals in total. Sources: the 2022 article and the Phase 2 supplementary methods. No Phase 2 case reinspection; two supplementary claims are recorded under the neural packet.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 5 male rhesus in total; 2 stimulated; 3 awake comparators; independent session counts unknown, since per-run volume counts and overlapping analysis windows are not run totals by animal and condition.
  • Findings: high central-thalamic stimulation raised the behavioral arousal score from 0 of 11 to 9 of 11 in both animals, low stimulation reached 3 of 11, ventrolateral control remained 0 of 11, and awake animals score 11 of 11; fMRI dynamics shifted and hierarchical auditory responses were enhanced; each animal's high setting was selected empirically just above its behavioral threshold, limiting any dose-response reading.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: not applicable; awareness is inferred from signatures, since macaques give no trialwise reports.
  • Unknowns: supplement run and exclusion details; blinding and randomized condition order, not established in the inspected text; the auditory paradigm has no ventrolateral control arm.
  • Classification: documented findings, the arousal scores and imaging results at their units; interpretation, awareness from arousal markers; unknown, the run totals.

C-T09-003: the shared-cohort anesthesia and sleep study

Forty healthy young men recruited; 39 anesthetized under randomized open-label propofol (19) or dexmedetomidine (20); 37 returned for a sleep arm on average eighteen weeks later; overlapping arms, not 76 participants. Source: the 2021 article. No Phase 2 case reinspection; two Phase 2 claims record the appendix search.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 40 recruited; 39 anesthesia; 19 and 20 by drug; 37 sleep returners; experience-report integers unknown, with subjective experience reported in 80 percent of anesthesia interviews and 71 percent of sleep interviews including contentless impressions, and a targeted search of the extended-data appendices finding regional PET tables rather than interview-count tables.
  • Findings: PET contrasts locate state-linked thalamic, cingulate and angular blood-flow changes as associations within manipulated states; the authors note that absent response or absent recalled content cannot conclusively establish absent experience; three awareness-report states were excluded from the comparison.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: immediate interviews where possible; none in deep unarousable states.
  • Unknowns: exact interview denominators and raw reports.
  • Classification: documented findings, the cohort counts and PET associations; uncorroborated testimony, the experience reports; interpretation, the disconnection reading; unknown, the integers.

C-T09-004: the single-patient fusiform stimulation case

One 45-year-old epilepsy patient in whom electrical stimulation of a right fusiform face-selective region pair elicited reported face distortions on seven real-face trials, while four sham trials and three trials stimulating a nearby control pair did not. Sources: the 2012 study and its 2013 correction, which changes only an author name. No Phase 2 reinspection.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 1 participant, aged 45; 7 real-face trials, 4 sham, 3 control-pair; famous-face and place naming tests present but not extracted as counts.
  • Findings: the sham and control contrasts support a causal contribution within this case; nonspecific nonface changes also occurred; the two stimulated regions were not separated; current can affect connected sites; the small naming tests differed procedurally and their null differences support no equivalence; randomization and assessor masking not established; one patient's repeated trials are not independent people; no unitary face center follows.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: reports at the time of stimulation; the video was not independently viewed.
  • Unknowns: as listed; the local-versus-network question.
  • Classification: documented findings, the trial outcomes as reported; interpretation, causal contribution to reported perceptual content; unknown, masking.

C-T13-001: the DMT and near-death-scale comparison, 2018

Thirteen healthy psychedelic-experienced volunteers measured after placebo and after DMT in a fixed placebo-first order with single blinding and heterogeneous doses, and a separate thirteen-person retrospectively recruited comparator selected as above the standard threshold; repeated conditions in thirteen people, not twenty-six, in the uncertain-overlap dependence group with the IANDS collections. Source: the 2018 article in full HTML without supplement or participant-level data. No Phase 2 reinspection.

  • Denominators, preserved at source units: 13 experimental people, 2 conditions each; 13 at or above the threshold of 7 after DMT and 1 after placebo; 13 comparator people, selected above threshold with no raw boundary-score list; dose counts as recorded, 7 mg for 3 people, 14 mg for 4, 18 mg for 1 and 20 mg for 5; time-item response-category counts not supplied; eligible or approached totals unknown.
  • Findings: time-item means 1.15 (standard deviation 0.80) under DMT and 1.54 (0.78) in the comparator, with non-significance not an equivalence test; published comparator sex counts (7 women, 6 men) differ from the experimental group's (6 women, 7 men) despite described matching.
  • Report timing versus corroboration: immediate scale completion in the experiment; retrospective in the comparator, with delay unspecified.
  • Witness independence: comparator overlap with the 2014 comparison or other Liège and IANDS studies unestablished; sample sizes are not to be added.
  • Unknowns: participant-level data; separately reported EEG; exact pair matching.
  • Classification: documented findings, the scale results at their units; uncorroborated testimony, the comparator's recollections; interpretation, endogenous-DMT causation or altered access, both withheld; unknown, overlap.

Read together, the twenty-three tables show why chapter 6 reached the verdict it did and why chapter 11's first three sensitivity tests behave as they do. Not one named case supplies documented findings for the conjunction that discrimination requires. The prospective studies supply documented flow counts and one chart-verified recollection but no confirmed opportunity denominator, and the AWARE II EEG units are documented findings about rhythms during resuscitation that are linked to no participant's report. The historical and cultural records supply dated recurrence under heavy literary and elicitation dependence. The controlled studies supply documented counts, arousal scores, imaging associations and trial outcomes at their units. The readings that turn those into awareness, disconnection or causal contribution to experience sit in the interpretation cell of each table, where the audit put them, and this appendix does not promote them. The classification rows are the audit's product, not the thesis's conclusion, and a reader who disagrees with a classification can find the record it rests on. Nothing in this appendix upgrades a report to an event, and nothing in it upgrades an interpretation to a finding.

BAlternatives matrix

This appendix sets the six families of positions side by side under the same six questions, and it does so under a rule that must be stated before any cell is read. Every comparison cell below is a project reconstruction. It was authored at the synthesis stage from listed accepted anchors, and only the exact statements of those anchors are reviewed author attributions. No cell is an author's own verdict on his or her position, no cell count is a score, and no row is ranked against another. The synthesis constraints require each cell to be marked as an inspected original, a secondary attribution, a project reconstruction or an uninspected variant, and they forbid any broad strongest-family ranking while the original-defender gaps in Appendix C remain open. The first withheld gate stands on exactly this appendix. The six questions are: coherence (C1); explanatory scope (C2); independent warrant and cost (C3); fit with the inspected evidence (C4); what could discriminate (C5); and remaining problems and the stopping point (C6). Entries under C5 are proposed test criteria, not demonstrated predictions.

Three things should be understood about the cells before any of them is read. First, the C1 to C6 cells below are the synthesis-stage reconstructions, printed as frozen version 1 text. They were written before Phase 2 supplied inspected originals and actual replies for several families, so a cell may state a family in a weaker form than chapter 3's strongest inspected formulation. For that reason the cells may not be used to grade any family against chapter 3. Chapter 3 carries the comparison at that level. The coverage line of each row below records what chapter 3 records about which exchanges are inspected originals, which positions are inspected without full reply chains, which remain secondary attributions, and which exact versions remain uninspected. Better coverage narrows a reconstruction's distance from the authors; it does not convert a reconstruction into an authored conclusion. Second, the purpose labels in the creator row follow the accepted correction from the synthesis stage: CF0 is production, CF1 is intentional production, and development and preservation are the further specified ends. A different reading of CF0, as the descriptive occurrence of conscious life with no purpose prediction, appears in two places. The accepted purpose hypotheses of the meaning packet state it, and the submitted P2S01 analysis reuses it. Where this appendix leans on P2S01's use of that reading, it is labeled as a rebased input, closed at its rebased scope by the release adjudication and never an accepted finding. Third, one cell has changed against the synthesis-stage text, and only one: the creator row's C5 cell dropped production from its list of tested purpose variants, and it is restored below to the accepted wording. The families overlap. Created persons can have a physical, psychophysical or dualist ontology; mental grounding can be plural or universal; a creator need not be identical with created persons; and the moral theories cross-cut all six.

Physical identity and full physical grounding

Coverage: Papineau's identity and phenomenal-concept defenses are inspected originals without a full reply chain; the illusionism exchange (Frankish, Nida-Rümelin, Frankish's reply) is an inspected original exchange added in Phase 2, with the eprint label not certified as publication equivalence; strong emergence is not this family by stipulation. The anchors for every cell are the Papineau attributions and the project's identity analysis, with the neural and case-inference claims added under fit and discrimination.

  • C1: different concepts can refer to one property; a positive identity warrant is needed, not merely the absence of contradiction. Project reconstruction from CL-T05-003, CL-T05-004, CL-T12-001 and CL-T12-003.
  • C2: the family can unify embodiment and experience if realization succeeds; physics and its laws remain a stopping point, and cosmic intention and value are separate additions. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C3: it avoids extra basic experiential laws by stipulation, but owes a realization or identity account, subject boundaries, and a justified causal-completeness premise if one is used. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C4: it fits the selected causal neural contributions; no accepted case establishes the process exclusion needed to refute a specified model; phenomenal adequacy remains contested. Project reconstruction adding CL-T07-016, CL-T09-019, CL-T09-020 and CL-T19-021.
  • C5: fix the necessary realization conditions before testing excluded-process experience; content-specific interventions matter, but report proxies must remain explicit. Project reconstruction adding CL-T09-001, CL-T09-002 and CL-T19-021.
  • C6: why this organization is experiential remains disputed; the comparison may not demand an explanation of every conscious primitive while permitting physical primitives without one. Project reconstruction from the core anchors.

Basic psychophysical laws and property dualism

Coverage: Chalmers's naturalistic dualism and Papineau's causal and epistemic objection are inspected originals; the causal-emergence exchange (O'Connor and Wong, Kim, Shoemaker) is an inspected original exchange added in Phase 2, which supplies the strong-emergence wing that the synthesis-stage matrix recorded as absent; a Cartesian two-way interactionism remains without an inspected defense of its own. The anchors are the psychophysical-law claims and the non-detachability and exclusion analyses.

  • C1: irreducible properties can lawfully depend on physical organization; closure plus causal mental relevance raises exclusion and epiphenomenal issues. Project reconstruction from CL-T12-005, CL-T12-006, CL-T12-007 and CL-T12-008.
  • C2: the family addresses experiential properties and neural mapping while leaving the basic laws and concrete existence unexplained; no detachable person follows. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C3: it adds experiential primitives and law mappings; economy depends on general constraints and successful application, not on the primitives being extra. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C4: a psychophysical-law model can expect neural dependence and is not refuted by interventions merely because experience is fundamental; retrospective anomalies remain underdetermined. Project reconstruction adding CL-T07-016, CL-T09-019, CL-T09-020 and CL-T19-021.
  • C5: prespecified mappings across systems could discriminate law variants; arbitrary mappings fitted separately to each case earn no predictive credit. Project reconstruction from the core anchors.
  • C6: why these laws, bounded subjects, and reliable experience-based beliefs under closure remain burdens; primitive laws receive the same stopping-rule test as identity and idealism. Project reconstruction from the core anchors.

Experiential constitution and panpsychist variants

Coverage: Chalmers's constitutive Russellian discussion and combination critique are inspected in an undated forthcoming manuscript; the constitution exchange (Strawson, Goff, Strawson's reply) is an inspected original exchange added in Phase 2, with the exact 2006 journal text never inspected and the later authorial version standing in as one work-version family; broad defender and reply coverage remains absent. The anchors are the combination-problem claims and the intrinsic-nature analysis.

  • C1: experiential role-realizers may avoid an entirely nonexperiential origin, but many micro-subjects do not automatically constitute one macro-subject. Project reconstruction from CL-T12-009, CL-T12-010, CL-T12-011 and CL-T12-012.
  • C2: the family can connect phenomenality and physical causal roles; ubiquity of experience alone does not explain existence, human survival or cosmic purpose. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C3: it adds a constitutive or bonding relation with subject, quality and structure constraints; whole-first variants exchange combination for decomposition. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C4: it can recover neural effects through experiential realization of roles, with the specific mapping still owed; neither near-death motifs nor intervention facts select ubiquitous experience. Project reconstruction adding CL-T07-016, CL-T08-008, CL-T08-012, CL-T09-019, CL-T09-020 and CL-T19-021.
  • C5: an explicit rule predicting subject boundaries, integration and dissociation would constrain the proposal; access to micro-subjectivity remains difficult. Project reconstruction from the core anchors.
  • C6: primitive experiential bases and constitution remain stopping points; the whole-first variant overlaps universal idealism, and protoexperience needs a fresh fundamentality assessment rather than inherited credit. Project reconstruction from the core anchors.

Distinct irreducible subjects and substance dualism

Coverage: Swinburne's short identity-bearer argument is an inspected original at the identity stage; the exclusion exchange (Lowe, Engelhardt including his interactive inclusive variant, Bennett) is an inspected original exchange added in Phase 2, with the Engelhardt text inspected through an author-linked transcription; the developed interactionist defender and reply chain beyond these remains open. The anchors are the simple-subject claims and the exclusion analysis.

  • C1: a distinct bearer can model persistence, but interaction or one-way dependence needs explicit laws; epistemic underdetermination does not itself prove an immaterial entity. Project reconstruction from CL-T13-005, CL-T13-006 and CL-T12-008.
  • C2: the family offers a bearer for subject unity and possible survival; distinctness neither supplies actuality beyond death nor explains all physical existence. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C3: it adds individual subjects, interaction or dependence laws and persistence conditions; emergent irreducible subjects need not satisfy fundamentality. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C4: neural dependence can be interface dependence if the interface has independently fixed constraints; neither generic possibility nor retelling establishes that account. Project reconstruction adding CL-T07-016, CL-T09-019, CL-T09-020 and CL-T19-021.
  • C5: prespecify when subject activity can proceed without specified neural processes and how content enters reports; assess timing and ordinary access before any anomaly credit. Project reconstruction adding CL-T09-001, CL-T09-002 and CL-T19-021.
  • C6: causal and epistemic fit and survival conditions remain open; a full original-family ranking is withheld rather than won by defeating a project straw version. Project reconstruction from the core anchors.

Mental grounding, plural or universal

Coverage: Kastrup's 2018 article, inspected through an identified journal-layout transcription, and Chalmers's idealism analysis, inspected in draft and final layout with unresolved publication history, are inspected originals without a verified direct reply chain; the plural comparator (Leibniz's Monadology and New System, Foucher's reply, Leibniz's explanation) is an inspected original exchange added in Phase 2 in abbreviated translations; Kastrup's clinical and physical premises remain secondary attributions; the developed plural-idealist primary chain remains absent. The anchors are the cosmic-idealism and shared-world claims.

  • C1: distinct finite perspectives can depend on one whole without all being identical to it; the psychological dissociation analogy does not establish cosmic individuation. Project reconstruction from CL-T12-013 through CL-T12-018.
  • C2: one conscious basis could unify subjectivity, public lawfulness and concrete grounding; survival, timelessness, intention and goodness are independent additions. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C3: a mental primitive plus grounding, appearance, law and individuation relations; unification is valuable only if the same constraints do several jobs instead of renaming separate puzzles. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C4: brain changes can be changes in mentally grounded relations, with the exact intervention mapping still needed; shared phenomenology does not by itself prove a universal subject. Project reconstruction adding CL-T07-016, CL-T08-008, CL-T08-012, CL-T09-019, CL-T09-020 and CL-T19-021.
  • C5: fix manifestation and individuation rules that could fail under neural and perceptual contrasts; an unconstrained appearance vocabulary cannot win by accommodating every outcome. Project reconstruction adding CL-T09-001, CL-T09-002 and CL-T19-021.
  • C6: why this concrete history and these laws, and why these private subjects, remain; plural grounding need not satisfy universal subjecthood, universal grounding can, and a nonintentional universal ground lacks intention. Project reconstruction from the core anchors.

Conscious creator with separately specified good authority

Coverage: the Leibniz texts, the two Collins versions, Boethius and Craig, the divine-command transcript, the restoration and hiddenness originals, and now the full modern cosmological exchange, the stringency chain, the complete eternity exchange and Schellenberg's actual 1996 response are inspected originals, each at its recorded version; no full modern sufficient-reason, design or theodicy comparison exists in the record, and distinct versions retain their own claims. Within chapter 3's own inputs the family was present mainly by stipulation; its developed content is carried by chapters 5, 7, 9 and 10. The anchors are the sufficient-reason, eternity and divine-nature claims.

  • C1: a timeless ground with temporal persons is a conditional option; perfect knowledge under objective tense and good governance require separate coherent specifications. Project reconstruction from CL-T10-004, CL-T10-005, CL-T10-006, CL-T10-009, CL-T11-007, CL-T15-001, CL-T15-002, CL-T15-023 and CL-T15-024.
  • C2: the family could unify existence, order, conscious value and cosmic purpose; the created-person ontology may be physical, psychophysical or dualist; survival requires an additional provision. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C3: it adds agent preferences, actual grounding, good nature, authority and possibly restoration; necessary existence and unlimited power and knowledge are not free properties of every variant. Project reconstruction from the same anchors.
  • C4: order and life may favor independently motivated intentions; suffering and hiddenness challenge the specified perfect-power and perfect-love variants; neural dependence of created persons is compatible. Project reconstruction adding CL-T07-016, CL-T09-019, CL-T09-020, CL-T10-018, CL-T15-006, CL-T15-013 and CL-T19-021.
  • C5: prespecify purpose, population, means and tradeoffs; production, intentional production, development and preservation face different tests; unrestricted hidden reasons weaken differential expectations. Project reconstruction adding CL-T14-015 and CL-T14-017, with the purpose labels restored to the accepted synthesis-stage wording (CF0 production, CF1 intentional production, CF2 development, CF3 preservation).
  • C6: why that agent, nature and intention, and why legitimate authority, remain; the creator need not be numerically identical to persons, and the creator variant can satisfy universal subjecthood in its created-subject domain. Project reconstruction from the core anchors.

The moral overlay

The six ontological families are not the four moral-grounding families, and the matrix carries a separate overlay for the moral comparison. At the synthesis stage the inspected positive originals were Craig's divine-nature account and Railton's naturalism, with the Rowe, Adams, Sutherland, Schellenberg and Howard-Snyder texts as inspected challenges and replies, and the developed constructivist and non-natural realist originals recorded as uninspected. Phase 2 inspected Korsgaard's chapters with Nagel's objection and her reply, and Enoch's first and fourth chapters and one section of the fifth with Lenman's critique and Enoch's reply; the morality chapter records both with their exact extents and with Enoch's developed indispensability argument still missing. The blocked conclusion for this overlay is unchanged: that all strong moral alternatives are eliminated, or that a physicalist ontology entails nihilism. The second withheld gate stands on it.

Common rules and the Phase 2 update

Four rules govern every cell. Every comparison cell is a project reconstruction, with original attribution claims separately addressable through their anchor records. The six ontological families are not the four moral families. No total ordering and no numeric posterior is performed, and equal open labels are not equal credibility. And a basic entity or law may be an explanatory stopping point in any family, so independent adjustable properties and rules are counted symmetrically. The submitted P2S01 analysis, used here as a labeled rebased input and not as an accepted finding, updated all thirty-six cells against the Phase 2 packets with explicit before-and-after records, applied the neural and controlled-acquisition constraints symmetrically, and selected no six-family winner; the submitted P2S02 execution, likewise labeled, retained the thirty-six cells under both original-restriction conditions as project comparisons. The cells printed above are not that update; they are the frozen version 1 text, with the single C5 correction stated above, and the P2S01 rewrite of them stands outside this appendix. The release adjudication closed that analysis at its rebased scope without promoting its artifacts to accepted findings (state/acceptance_P3G2.json), and the cells stay the frozen version 1 text under the rule stated at the head of this appendix, which rests on a recorded owner decision (D5, cited in the crosswalk entries for apB-p002 and for this paragraph). Nothing in either analysis, and nothing in this appendix, ranks the families.

CGap and withheld-gate disposition register

This register does one thing that the rebase amendment requires and that no chapter can do for itself. It gives every one of the thirty-five historical gap groups and every one of the twelve withheld stronger-conclusion gates exactly one explicit disposition. The amendment allows three kinds: resolved by cited reviewed evidence, addressed by a bounded Phase 3 gap packet with its outcome recorded, or a standing limitation stated in the relevant chapter. A generic caveat does not satisfy a row, and an administrative status never closes an evidence gap. Two facts about this register should be understood before reading it. First, no Phase 3 gap packet was dispatched, because no chapter attempted a conclusion that a packet would have been needed to license; so no row carries the second kind of disposition. Second, the dispositions below are the author's proposals to the coverage gate. Only the overseer records a disposition, and only the coverage gate can accept coverage; nothing in this appendix closes a gap or opens a gate. The coverage gate adopted these dispositions on 14 September 2026 after mechanical verification of every named paragraph (state/acceptance_P3G1.json; work/P3G1/disposition_register.json). A later semantic re-check of every row narrowed or re-pointed eight paragraph lists and added the missing statement to four chapter paragraphs, recorded as amendment P3G1-A1 of the same record; no disposition changed. The gate's record, not this appendix, is the disposition of record.

Each gap row is built from three layers. The historical wording of the gap, its closure condition and its priority come from the canonical register fixed at the synthesis audit. The Phase 2 status comes from the accepted methods audit, which split most gaps into narrow subrequirements and recorded, for each, whether it was resolved, partly addressed, access-unresolved, deferred with the stronger claim withheld, already resolved, or a persistent audit loss; those audit labels describe requirement statuses, not evidential weights. The thesis disposition then names the category and the chapter paragraphs where the limitation is actually stated, so that the coverage gate can check the statement rather than take it on trust. Three rows are proposed as resolved by cited reviewed evidence at their inspected scope. Thirty-one are proposed as standing limitations stated in a chapter. One, the historical audit loss, is a standing limitation that no repair can ever close, and it is stated as such in the methodology chapter.

Gap groups

The Phase 2 reinspection resolved the narrow subrequirements that public sources could resolve and left the rest access-unresolved or deferred with the stronger claim withheld.

Thesis disposition: standing limitation, stated in chapter 6 at c06-p012 and c06-p044. Closure would require the exact earliest edition and independently dated disclosure evidence; if unavailable, priority remains unknown.
  • G1-GAP-02, independent rescue statements and measured physiological chronology. Thesis disposition: standing limitation, stated at c06-p013 and c06-p014. Closure would require public, independently dated witness or medical documentation, without any private-record request.

The historical audit loss

One gap can never be closed, and the register says so. Lost early search work, mixed pilot ranks and a lost combined query cannot be reconstructed as historical facts; any replacement search carries a new date and repairs nothing about the old capture. The first names the lost T03 search work, the mixed pilot batches with unrecoverable per-query ranks and the uncaptured T05 combined OR query as historical facts; the second states that no later search reconstructs them. No closure condition exists; the disclosure is retained.

Thesis disposition: standing limitation, stated at c06-p028, c06-p029 and c06-p044, where the 140 versus 142 discrepancy and the unresolved 74 percent denominator are preserved.

Priority P1; Phase 2 deferred with the stronger claim withheld (typed arm links prevent cross-arm attribution; the intersections remain missing; 47 percent refers to EEG data, not people). Thesis disposition: standing limitation, stated at c06-p030, c06-p031 and c06-p034. Closure would require source data with defined trial opportunities and linked timing; unknown remains unknown, never zero.

For the coverage gate
